| **LIFE-EX-PLAN-5** | **A question node PRE-AUTHORS the continuation that follows resolution, in the same call.** A graded turn used to cost TWO `life_step` calls: the pick-resolving call that emits the question node (measured: ~15,400 input tokens for **93 output tokens** — a `gradedPlan` and nothing else), then a second full-context call after the player answers, to narrate what happens next. The second call is now eliminated: the question node carries `resolution: { beats?, options, logLine? }`, authored by the same call that planned the question, and `feedbackContinue` renders it from memory. **This is safe because `LIFE-CONTRACT-EX-3` already forbids the narrative from forking on correctness** — "the speech event happens identically regardless of correctness … the `practiceOutcome { correct }` is for OPTIONAL soft acknowledgement only — it MUST NOT fork the narrative". There is therefore only ONE continuation to author, not a correct-path and a wrong-path, so nothing is generated speculatively and no branch is discarded (contrast `LIFE-NODE-2`, where lookahead was removed precisely because 2 of 3 branches were always thrown away). The pedagogical response to a wrong answer is unaffected: it is `whyWrong`, authored per-chip by the generator and rendered inline at zero model cost (`LIFE-EX-GEN-9`/`-11`). **What is given up** is the optional soft acknowledgement ("you stumbled but Sophie understood"), which `LIFE-CONTRACT-EX-3` marks optional and which the pre-authored continuation must therefore not attempt. **Bonus correctness:** `LIFE-CONTRACT-EX-4` (never re-emit the just-practised line) becomes structurally easier to obey, because the call that authors `sentenceContext` / `npcUtterance` also authors the follow-on and cannot re-quote a line it can no longer see. **Graceful degradation:** `resolution` is OPTIONAL on the wire. When it is absent (a model that omits it, a card-match review which has no plan — `LIFE-EX-10`, or a graded node reached by demotion), `feedbackContinue` falls back to the prefetched live turn exactly as before, so this can only ever remove a call, never break the flow. Measured 2026-07: 4 of 14 `life_step` calls in a 10-node run were post-feedback continuations at ~$0.0032 each; pre-authoring costs ~450 output tokens (~$0.0011) and saves ~$0.0021 per graded turn, ~18% of `life_step` spend and 29% fewer calls — and the Continue tap becomes instant instead of awaiting a prefetch. |
| **LIFE-EX-GEN-1** | The graded-question generator is a SEPARATE Gemini call (`gemini_graded_question` telemetry category). It takes `{ plan: GradedPlan, practiceLanguage, nativeLanguage, recentTargets: string[] }` and emits `{ practicePrompt, choices }`. The SI is RUTHLESSLY focused: USER VIEW, UNIVERSAL RULES, ANSWER-LEAK RULES, DISAMBIGUATION (per the strategy in the plan), CHIP-SHAPE RULES. No narrative voice — the generator never authors story. |
| **LIFE-EX-GEN-2** | The disambiguation principle: every graded question MUST have exactly ONE clearly-correct chip. The generator achieves this through distractor choice and the surrounding practice-language context, WITHIN the fixed one-language chip rule (LIFE-LANG-1) — language placement is pinned per kind and is NOT a disambiguation lever. No structured disambiguation hint; the generator chooses the distractors that fit the moment. (Rewritten in the Wurlds-v2 Phase D commit; the pre-2026-07 body allowed per-chip language mixing.) |
| **LIFE-EX-GEN-3** | ANSWER-LEAK RULES (strict, enforced by a pre-render guard): (a) For comprehension, no chip's `practiceText` or `nativeText` may equal or be a substring of the `npcUtterance` (verbatim echoes give the answer away); additionally every chip's `nativeText` is REQUIRED non-empty and distinct, and `practicePrompt.question` is REQUIRED non-empty (LIFE-LANG-1/2). (b) For fill-blank, each chip's `practiceText` MUST be the bare missing token(s) only — never include template words. (c) Fill-blank CHIPS carry no native gloss at all: `nativeText` MUST be the empty string on every chip (LIFE-LANG-1) — a hard programmatic check. **The SENTENCE-level translation is separate and is now allowed** (`LIFE-LANG-17` / `LIFE-EX-GEN-9`): under `disambiguation: 'translation-anchor'` `completedNativeText` renders above the template pre-answer, and the guard instead checks that it does NOT contain the answer verbatim. Violations retry once with the hint attached; second failure → demote to CYOA. |
| **LIFE-EX-GEN-4** | The generator authors EXACTLY 3-4 chips. EXACTLY ONE has `correctness: 'correct'` and matches the planned target. Wrong chips are pedagogically meaningful, and **for fill-blank the distractor TYPE depends on the target + level** (playtest fix — the "every question is a conjugation drill" complaint): a **VOCABULARY target** (noun / adjective / common word — the norm at levels 1-4) gets DIFFERENT words of the same class that could grammatically fill the blank but are the WRONG meaning for the sentence (café → thé / eau / lait), testing "which word fits" — never three near-identical verb endings a beginner must split hairs over; a **VERB-FORM target** (a conjugation the player is drilling — reserved for higher levels, `LIFE-EX-PLAN-2`) gets inflectional variants of the SAME lemma (voudrais → veux / voulais / voudrai). EITHER WAY every distractor is a grammatically-plausible fill (so it stays fill-blank, NOT an unrelated-lemma CYOA in disguise) yet semantically / contextually wrong; the distractor type VARIES across questions. For comprehension, wrong chips are plausible-but-incorrect INTERPRETATIONS (third-person) — never first-person responses. |
| **LIFE-EX-GEN-9** | **DISAMBIGUATION is mandatory on fill-blank** — practice mode's `PRACTICE-GEN-10` lifted into the sim, which had no equivalent. Every distractor a competent generator writes is a real practice-language word that fits the slot grammatically; that is the point, and also the trap. `[peux / veux / doit]` all produce a good sentence, so nothing in the question says which meaning was wanted and the item has no discoverable answer. `practicePrompt.disambiguation` is therefore REQUIRED and is one of: **`context-anchor`** (the sentence carries enough context that only one option is coherent — preferred, the learner reasons about meaning) or **`translation-anchor`** (the completed sentence's system-language translation is SHOWN pre-answer and IS the discriminator — for modals, near-synonyms, and fixed expressions with several valid fillers). Every WRONG chip additionally carries `whyWrong`: one short clause saying why it is wrong IN THIS SENTENCE. It is a generator forcing-function (mirroring practice mode's forced `thinking` self-check) AND is SHOWN to the learner on picking that chip (`LIFE-EX-GEN-11`), so write it learner-facing. **Enforced by `leakViolations`**: a missing strategy, a missing `whyWrong`, or two distractors sharing the same reason (if they are wrong for the same reason they are probably interchangeable) each fail the guard → one retry with the hint → demote per `LIFE-EX-GEN-6`. A `translation-anchor` whose shown text contains the answer verbatim also fails — the question would answer itself. **NEVER a COGNATE testTarget (v2.1):** a word near-identical in the native language (plan→plan, documents, restaurant) teaches nothing AND makes the fill-blank UNANSWERABLE-as-authored — with `translation-anchor` the cognate answer inevitably appears in the shown native sentence ("What is their exact ___?" → "…exact plan?"), a self-leak the guard rejects. Both the step SI (planner, picks the testTarget) and the graded SI (author) are steered off cognates. **Every rejection is now LOGGED** (v2.1, `lifeFailed` — invalid JSON / schema / answer-leak per attempt, with the testTarget + violations, and the final `exhausted-retries: <last violation>` reason), so a snag is diagnosable from the debug buffer instead of silently retried. |
| **LIFE-EX-GEN-11** | **`whyWrong` is shown to the learner.** The graded generator already authors a one-line reason each wrong chip is wrong (`whyWrong`, `LIFE-EX-GEN-9/10`). It was previously discarded — used only as an authoring guard — while the learner had to tap **Why?** for a SECOND LLM call (`ExplainModal`) to get any explanation. It now renders inline in `PracticeFeedback` on an incorrect answer (`data-testid="LifeScreen-PracticeFeedback-WhyWrong"`, under the "you picked" row), giving immediate, error-specific feedback at ZERO model cost. The generator SI is told the clause is user-facing, so it writes to teach rather than as a terse internal note. Correct answers and legacy/absent reasons render nothing. **Why?** stays as the optional deeper dive. |
| **LIFE-EX-GEN-12** | **Graded chips must be DISTINCT — no two identical options.** A multiple-choice question whose visible options repeat (the beta bug: the correct word appearing as two chips) is unanswerable. `leakViolations` enforces distinctness on the RENDERED label per kind: comprehension checks `nativeText` (the visible label, `LIFE-LANG-1`, already present), **fill-blank checks `practiceText`** (added v2.1). A collision fails the guard → one retry with the hint → demote per `LIFE-EX-GEN-6` if it persists. |
| **LIFE-LANG-17** | **The native gloss on a graded fill-blank is OPTIONAL and generator-chosen**, superseding `LIFE-LANG-1`/`LIFE-EX-GEN-3(c)`'s blanket ban on any native text in the fill-blank surface. The ban was correct about CHIPS and wrong about the SENTENCE: a per-chip gloss labels each option with its answer, but the sentence-level translation gives away no single chip and is the only honest way to ask a question whose options are interchangeable without it. `PracticePromptCard` renders `completedNativeText` above the template (`data-testid="LifeScreen-PracticePrompt-Anchor"`, copy `life.practice.sayThis`) **iff** `disambiguation === 'translation-anchor'`. Under `context-anchor` — still the default and the richer exercise — nothing native is shown. Chips remain gloss-free in both cases. |
| **LIFE-LANG-18** | **RETIRED 2026-09-24 with swap words ([swap-words-removal.md](./swap-words-removal.md)).** The step SI no longer asks for `swapWords[]`, no beat or chip carries it, and the Mixed row that rendered it is gone (`LIFE-LANG-3`). A cached step response or stored beat that still has a `swapWords` key parses and renders exactly as if it were absent. *Was:* **Two vocab lists on every practice line — `complexWords` AND `swapWords`.** `LIFE-LANG-10`'s segmentation produces `complexWords[]` + `wordTranslations{}`, covering the whole line. `swapWords[]` is a SUBSET of `complexWords` naming the entries that are genuine CODE-SWITCH candidates: the practice word or phrase a bilingual speaker would actually drop into a system-language sentence, because it fits the moment better or the system-language word does not come to them. The test is whether it would land as completely natural — logically AND grammatically — inside the system-language sentence with the surrounding grammar unchanged. Flag concrete nouns, vivid verbs and self-contained fixed expressions; never function words, never an inflected form whose grammar depends on its host sentence, never numbers / dates / proper nouns / obvious cognates. **Zero is a perfectly good answer** — this list is a curation on top of a segmentation, and the two rules must not contaminate each other: `complexWords` is complete coverage with no judgement (`LIFE-LANG-10`), `swapWords` is judgement with no coverage obligation. An entry in `swapWords` that is absent from `complexWords` is DROPPED at the schema seam rather than trusted, exactly as `MSG-PIPE-17.2` does for chat — a swap word with no gloss has nothing to show. Carried end-to-end on the same path as the other two (`ZStepBeat` → `Bubble` → `ZLifeBeatPayload` → `resumeBubblesFromEvents`), so a resumed bubble keeps it. **No render surface consumes it yet** — chat's Easy mode (`MSG-VOCAB-2`) injects swap words into the NATIVE sentence, and a wurld is practice-first by default (`LIFE-LANG-3` `hidden`), so the only coherent home is the `native-only` view mode. That is a product decision, deliberately NOT taken here; the data is produced and persisted so the render is a small isolated change when wanted. |
| **LIFE-EX-GEN-5** | The generator output is parsed against the existing `PracticePrompt` + `StepChoice` schemas — same shapes, same constraints, same downstream rendering. The split is internal: the API contract back to the UI (`StepResponse`) is unchanged. |
| **LIFE-EX-GEN-6** | Generator failure modes: (a) Gemini call throws / times out → demote to CYOA (drop graded:true, drop practicePrompt, ship a fallback "Take a moment" beat with the same chips the lifeStep would have authored on a non-graded turn). (b) schema parse fails → one retry with hint, then demote. (c) leak guard fails → one retry with hint, then demote. No infinite retry loops; the player never sees a broken graded turn. |
| **LIFE-EX-GEN-7** | The generator receives a TIGHT scene context — saga title (1 line), current location label, the speaker's name when relevant, and the last 2-3 narrative beats. Without it the generator invents distractors from generic templates ("not enough money in the register") that clash with the actual world ("battling alien drones"). It does NOT need the full lifeStep context (no scene outline, no NPC roster, no recent events log) — just enough setting to anchor the chip set in the world's register. |
| **LIFE-EX-GEN-8** | The generator SHUFFLES the authored chip set (Fisher-Yates, `shuffleChoices(choices, rng = Math.random)` in `src/lib/life/shuffleChoices.ts`) before returning, so the correct answer's POSITION in the chip grid is randomised. The model authors the correct chip first (its OUTPUT SHAPE example lists the "correct" chip as c1); without the shuffle the answer is always the first chip — a trivial positional tell. `correctness` identifies the answer independent of order, so shuffling preserves the exact element set and the single `correctness: 'correct'` chip while scrambling its index. The shuffle runs AFTER the leak-guard (`LIFE-EX-GEN-3`) so guard diagnostics still reference authoring order. |

### Speech direction — `LIFE-EX-DIR-*`

Reported 2026-07-31: *"a generated question confuses the who said what … make sure the question direction is decided once, eg npc talking or me talking, and that this governs what is being said … and mechanically the display of the message after, attributed properly to an npc, or to me and never the wrong one or both."*

The direction was never MISSING — it was **implied three times over and stated nowhere the model could observe it**, which is the defect class the rest of this spec keeps naming. `gradedPlan.kind` implied it (`fill-blank` ⇒ the player speaks, `comprehension` ⇒ an NPC does). `buildResolvedBubble` re-derived it from `practicePrompt.kind`. The generator was told neither: the graded SI described the LAYOUT of each kind ("the sentence with the blank", "the NPC's verbatim line") and never once said WHOSE MOUTH the line comes out of, so a fill-blank template authored in an NPC's voice was a perfectly compliant generation — and landed, mechanically, in the player's own bubble. `LIFE-SPEECH-1`'s `say`→`fill-blank` / `hear`→`comprehension` lock governs the PLANNER, one call earlier; nothing carried it into the authoring call.

`LIFE-EX-DIR-*` collapses those implications into ONE typed value.

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| **LIFE-EX-DIR-1** | **The direction is decided ONCE, in code, before generation — and it is a value, not a convention.** `gradedDirection(plan)` (`src/lib/life/gradedQuestionEngine.ts` — it stayed CLIENT-side when the prompt moved in SRV-PROMPT-8..13 batch 7, because the client is what attributes the resolved bubble; the server re-derives the same bit from the same `plan.kind` to render `<direction>`) is a TOTAL function from the `gradedPlan` to a discriminated union: `{ speaker: 'player' }` for `fill-blank`, `{ speaker: 'npc'; speakerName: string \| null; npcId: string \| null }` for `comprehension`. The speaker IDENTITY rides on the `npc` arm, so "an NPC is speaking but nobody knows who" is representable only as an explicit `null` rather than as two independent optional fields that may or may not have been populated. `plan.kind` is the source because it is ALREADY the direction — `ZGradedPlan` derives it from whose line the plan holds at the parse seam (`LIFE-EX-18`), so a plan reaching this function cannot have a `kind` its fields disagree with — so this names an existing decision instead of adding a competing one. Nothing downstream may re-derive the direction from the model's output; everything reads this value. (Until `LIFE-EX-18` the pin came from the picked option's `speechAct` instead: same single-source shape, wrong source.) |
| **LIFE-EX-DIR-2** | **The direction is an INPUT to the generator prompt, stated unambiguously, and the model may not restate it.** `buildGradedQuestionPrompt` renders a `<direction speaker="player">` / `<direction speaker="npc" name="…">` block from `gradedDirection(plan)` — computed, never re-typed — that names the voice, the addressee, and the CONSEQUENCE ("the completed sentence is rendered verbatim inside the PLAYER'S OWN bubble"). The SI carries the matching `SPEAKER DIRECTION` section: the block is decided by the engine, is not a suggestion, and a line written in the other voice will be displayed under the wrong speaker. **The model is told NOT to author `speakerName` / `npcId` at all** — they are engine-set. The former OUTPUT SHAPE hint `"speakerName": "<from plan>"` was the drift vector `LIFE-CANON-4` describes at the beat level: a prose instruction to copy a value the client already holds, which the model was free to paraphrase, translate, or invent, and which `PracticePromptCard` then rendered ("Sophie said:") beside a bubble the engine attributed from the PLAN. Two sources for one fact is the bug. |
| **LIFE-EX-DIR-3** | **A generated prompt is reconciled against the direction: identity is NORMALISED, voice is REJECTED.** `applyGradedDirection(direction, prompt)` overwrites a comprehension prompt's `speakerName`/`npcId` with the direction's values and discards whatever the model wrote. This is a normalisation, not a rejection, because the correct value is already known in code — a retry could only reproduce it, so spending one would be pure cost (same reasoning as `LIFE-SPEECH-1`'s repair-don't-retry). `directionViolations(direction, prompt)` is the REJECT half, for what cannot be normalised: (a) a `kind` that disagrees with the direction; (b) a fill-blank template written as attributed narration — `hasAttributionWrapper` (`src/lib/life/utterance.ts`), the same deterministic detector `LIFE-CONTRACT-BUBBLE-4` already runs on `npcUtterance`, now run on the outbound template too, because "« … », dit Sophie" as a template is exactly the line that ends up in the player's bubble. A violation joins `leakViolations` → one retry with the hint → demote per `LIFE-EX-GEN-6`. **Known limit, deliberately not papered over:** the detector fires on ATTRIBUTED prose, not on a plain third-person sentence ("Sophie prépare le ___"). Catching that needs the addressee on the plan, which `LIFE-EX-PLAN-2` does not carry; the `<direction>` block is the primary contract and the guard is the backstop, in that order. |
| **LIFE-EX-DIR-4** | **A persisted beat's attribution is COHERENT with its speaker — never both, never neither.** `commitNode` clears `speakerName` + `npcId` on every non-`npc` beat before `appendBeatEvent`, at the one chokepoint every Firestore beat write passes through. A `user` beat carrying an `npcId` is the "attributed to an NPC AND to me" state the report names; it also poisons what the model reads NEXT turn, since `<recentEvents>` binds a beat's line to its `npcId` row (`LIFE-CTX-10a`) and `LIFE-CANON-4` then has two candidate speakers for one line. The pass is idempotent and matches the render exactly — `DialogueArea` routes on `bubble.speaker` alone and a `user` bubble never reads either field — so the persisted event and the on-screen bubble cannot diverge (`LIFE-BEAT-REPAIR`'s standing requirement). |

**Repair path, FIXED 2026-07-31 (was an open defect).** `gradedPlanDirectionFix` returns the corrected `kind`, and the caller used to apply it as `{ ...gradedPlan, kind: fixedKind }` — a spread carrying the OLD variant's body across. Repairing a `comprehension` plan onto a `say` pick yielded `{ kind: 'fill-blank', npcUtterance }` with no `testTarget`, which is not a `GradedPlan` at all; the cast to the node type hid it and `buildGradedQuestionPrompt` threw a `TypeError` on the missing field. Because `authorQuestion` fires the generator as an un-caught `void` promise, the node then sat in `question-authoring` FOREVER — the repair created the hang it existed to avoid. `repairGradedPlan` now RECONSTRUCTS the body per variant and the runner checks `gradedPlanIssues` before building the prompt, so a malformed plan demotes (`LIFE-EX-GEN-6`) instead of hanging. Asserted in both directions by `generateStepOptions.test.ts`.

---

## 10.46 Node step engine — `LIFE-NODE-*`

The turn loop is a node graph the player walks. Every player-visible story moment is a NODE, and ONE Gemini `life_step` call renders the node the player just moved to, together with its outgoing options.

> **Lookahead previews were REMOVED 2026-07 (`LIFE-NODE-2`, cost audit).** The engine used to have every option carry a one-node-deep preview (`next` XOR `noPreview`) so a pick rendered instantly. Measurement killed it: previews were ~30% of `life_step` output tokens, **two of every three were discarded unread**, and — the decisive part — **they never saved a call.** A previewed pick still cost one `<turn kind="options">` call to author the new node's options; an unpreviewed pick cost one live call that returned node *and* options. Identical call count, ~20% more output on every single turn, paid to render 2 branches the player did not take. What previews bought was latency (instant render vs. a ~2-3s wait), and that is now spent explicitly in the `live-loading` state rather than implicitly in every turn's bill. See `docs/archive/model-migration-2026-07.md` §28.3.4.

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| **LIFE-NODE-1** | Node taxonomy: `branch` (a real decision — 0–2 beats, 2–3 model options, plus the client-injected ✏️ chip), `continuation` (connective pacing — 1–2 beats, exactly ONE option with id `continue`), `question` (graded teaching intercept — NO beats, a `gradedPlan` only; the chip set is authored by the `LIFE-EX-GEN-*` generator when the node is shown, phase `question-authoring`). A node is exactly one kind; the beat caps are schema-enforced. |
| **LIFE-NODE-2** | **ONE `life_step` call per player action, returning the node to render NOW plus its outgoing options. Options carry NO lookahead** — no `next`, no `noPreview`; the fields are gone from the schema. Every pick routes to a live `<turn kind="…">` generation. Max 3 model options per response. Turn-level signals (`worldDelta`, `scene`, `introduces`, `logLine`, `npcMemoryUpdates`) ride the response. **A `question` node carries NO options** — its chips come from the `LIFE-EX-GEN-*` generator when the node is shown — so the model must not author any, and a response that does is authoring output nobody reads. The `<turn kind="options">` request shape survives for exactly ONE caller: the graded-authoring demote path (`LIFE-NODE-8`), which needs branch options for a node already on screen. **Reserved exception, NOT implemented:** a `continuation` node has branching factor 1 — exactly one option, id `continue`, and no write-your-own chip (`LifeScreenBottom`: `nodeKind === 'branch' ? [...options, writeChip] : [...options]`) — so lookahead on THAT node kind would discard nothing and the rationale above does not apply to it. A full implementation design is in `docs/archive/model-migration-2026-07.md` §28.10, including the precondition to validate first: continuation nodes were measured at **0 of 25** on 2026-07-30 (`13 branch · 12 question`), and the design only breaks even above a ~8% continuation rate. Anyone implementing it must rewrite THIS invariant to state the exception rather than silently override it. |
| **LIFE-NODE-3** | Commit-on-arrival. On arrival at a node (always a live landing now — `LIFE-NODE-2`), `commitNode` writes in order: (1) the action event (`decision` / `correction`; none for opening/continue), (2) each beat as a `beat` event with NPC promotion + avatar kickoff interleaved per NPC beat, (3) the `currentBackdrop` patch from `node.backdropIntent`. `commitNode` + the in-memory overlay fire ONCE, immediately, on arrival — the beat-glide (`LIFE-NODE-9`) defers only the visual bubble render, never the commit. `worldDelta` + scene signals apply when the generating response lands (`applyTurnSignals` + the existing replan/hook/resolve paths). Kill mid-node is safe: the events log is the source of truth and the resume path is unchanged. Committed events are mirrored into an in-memory overlay so the immediately-following prompt sees them before the Firestore listener catches up. The overlay's dedup fingerprint (kind + primary text) falls back to `nativeText` when `practiceText` is empty: narrator beats carry empty `practiceText` under the two-channel model (`LIFE-LANG-8`), so a `practiceText`-only key collided ALL narrator beats and dropped just-committed narration from the overlay — the fallback keys them on their narration instead (`mergeLocalEvents`, engine-audit fix). |
| **LIFE-NODE-4** | The ✏️ "write your own" chip (`WRITE_YOUR_OWN_ID = '__write__'`) is CLIENT-injected as the 4th chip on branch nodes only — the model never emits it. Tapping it swaps the chip grid for an inline composer (cancellable); submit routes as `<turn kind="free">` live generation. The always-visible composer is deleted. Continuation and question nodes have no ✏️. |
| ~~**LIFE-NODE-5**~~ | **RETIRED 2026-07 (previews removed, `LIFE-NODE-2`).** Described the `noPreview` rules — the mandatory `noPreview` on the scene hook-accept chip, and the sanitize pass that demoted an individually-broken `next` rather than burning the response's retry. With no previews there is nothing to demote and nothing to except: EVERY pick routes to live generation, which is what the hook-accept chip always needed anyway. The hook-accept and resolution paths are unchanged. Removing the sanitize pass also removes a whole class of partial-response repair from `generateStepOptions`. |
| **LIFE-NODE-6** | Nonce invalidation, **now guarding in-flight results only** (the preview cache it also guarded is gone, `LIFE-NODE-2`). A monotonic client nonce bumps ONLY on (a) ✏️ free-text submit and (b) a `hardPivot` replan trigger; ordinary replans (soft tick, `replanRequested`) do NOT bump — one turn against the old outline is tolerated. A generation result whose nonce ≠ the current one is DROPPED, so a response authored against a superseded outline never reaches the screen. `cacheNonceRef` and the `cacheFresh` guard are deleted along with the cache: with every pick routing live there is no stale-preview case left to detect, and a hardPivot landing mid-turn is handled by the drop rule alone. Separately, `LifeScreen` is keyed by `worldId` in the router (`routes.tsx` `KeyedLifeScreen`) so a param-only world switch fully REMOUNTS the engine — no cross-world ref/state bleed (stale bubbles, foreign event overlay, wedged opening). |
| **LIFE-NODE-7** | Cadence: ~1 node in 3 is a question — enforced by the client counter directive (`decision` events since the last `correction` ≥ 2 injects `<practiceCadence>`). **Post-preview-removal the directive targets the node being emitted THIS turn** ("make THIS node a question with a gradedPlan"), not "put question previews on at least two options" — there are no previews to place them on, and asking for two was itself a hedge against the player picking the un-previewed branch. Fresh targets only (not in `<recentlyPracticed>`), with the same escape hatch: if every viable target has been practiced and no comprehension moment fits, stay narrative rather than recycle. The SI's QUESTION CADENCE line states "aim for roughly 1 node in 3 to be a question" — placed WHEREVER it naturally fits (the player about to read / say something, or an NPC about to say content), but never every turn (that breaks flow). Continuations are separately capped by SI guidance at ~1 in 4; real branches preferred. The live node after a `practiceOutcome` is NEVER a question (carried over from `LIFE-CONTRACT-EX-*`). |
| **LIFE-NODE-8** | Failure ladder — **a failed turn is now ATOMIC** (`LIFE-NODE-2`): pre-preview-removal a pick rendered its cached preview beats and committed the decision immediately, so a failed follow-up call left a half-turn on the record (beats + decision written, no options). With no lookahead there is nothing to render until the live call lands, so a failed turn commits NOTHING and the world is exactly as it was — the story did not advance and the event log agrees. The call retries once inside `generateStepOptions`, then the UI enters `options-failed` — an i18n'd note + a client-synthesized Continue chip; tapping it fires a live `<turn kind="continue">` generation. **Question-chip authoring failure GRACEFULLY DEMOTES (v2.1): instead of the `options-failed` ladder, the node authors normal CYOA options (`fetchOptions('branch')`) so the story just continues WITHOUT the quiz** — only a SECOND failure (the options gen itself) falls back to the snag ladder. This is the fix for valid-looking-but-rejected questions snagging: e.g. a name fill-blank whose `translation-anchor` shows `completedNativeText` "Hello Élodie…" which CONTAINS the answer "Élodie" is correctly rejected as a self-leak (`leakViolations`), but the player now sees choices, not a snag. Live-turn failure lands on the snag ladder. The player is never stuck and never sees a wrong-language fallback beat. |
| **LIFE-NODE-9** | Beat-glide advance animation. **Trigger changed 2026-07 (previews removed, `LIFE-NODE-2`):** there is no cached pick any more, so the glide now runs on the LIVE landing of a pick — `arriveAtNode` with `appendMode: 'reset'`, i.e. any arrival that follows a player action, as opposed to the opening node, question authoring and practice resolution, which keep their instant append. The animation itself is unchanged, and it matters MORE now, not less: the beats arrive after a visible `live-loading` wait, so staggering them in reads as the story unfolding rather than as a block of text appearing at once. The next node's beats GLIDE UP staggered from the bottom instead of appearing instantly: `arriveAtNode` first clears the turn boundary (`appendBubbles([], { resetTurn: true })`), then `scheduleBeatGlide` (`src/screens/LifeScreen/nodeEngineGlide.ts`) mounts each beat one at a time — a ~300ms pause before the first (lets the chip grid clear), then `BEAT_STAGGER_MS` between successive beats — deliberately ≥ the ~380ms glide (`GLIDE_ENTER_MS`) so beat N fully settles before beat N+1 starts rising; at the old 250ms (< glide) two beats overlapped mid-animation and read as "bunched" (playtest fix). `BEAT_STAGGER_MS` is no longer its own number — it IS `EVENT_PACING_MS` (`LIFE-NODE-11`), which raised it from 500ms to 1000ms. Each new bubble mounts and plays the `.life-beat-enter` glide (`@keyframes lifeBeatEnter`, ~380ms) as it appears — **extended to TWO axes by `LIFE-NODE-12`**: it rises 20px AND slides in from the speaker's own side. Newly-mounted bubbles carry the glide class; already-mounted bubbles keep their DOM node (keyed by id) and never re-animate. **The pause + stagger are VISUAL ONLY:** `commitNode` and the in-memory event overlay (`pushLocal`) still fire ONCE, immediately, unchanged — the next prompt sees the committed events and the `LIFE-NPC-6` roster sweep runs regardless of the glide. A monotonic `staggerGen` guard cancels a still-pending glide if a newer node arrives. `prefers-reduced-motion` skips the pause + stagger (instant append) and the CSS class drops the transform (opacity-only fade). |
| **LIFE-NODE-10** | Authoring-shimmer floor. The `question-authoring` shimmer is shown for exactly as long as the graded generator (`runGradedQuestionGenerate`) runs; the real generator's ~1.5s latency reads as an intentional "writing the question" beat, but a fast resolve (a future cache hit, or the mocked generator under test) would flash it for a few ms. `authorQuestion` therefore races the generator against a cosmetic floor `MIN_AUTHORING_MS` (=700ms, `nodeEngineGlide.ts`): it starts `authoringFloor()` when the shimmer mounts and `await`s it before transitioning to `question-ready`, so the shimmer is ALWAYS visible for at least the floor. Same pattern as the transit floor (`LIFE-TRANS-6`). The floor only bites when the generator is faster than it, so it never adds latency in production. The supersession guard (phase still `question-authoring`) is re-checked AFTER the floor so a mid-wait nonce/replan drop still cancels cleanly. |
| **LIFE-NODE-11** | **A turn arrives as ONE paced sequence: every event, then the options, each `EVENT_PACING_MS` (=1000ms) apart** (`nodeEngineBeats.ts`). Reported 2026-07-31 in two parts. **(1)** "When two events are generated in one turn, at least one second pause between, so they arrive visually separately." A node routinely carries 2-3 beats and each is its own event in the log, but at the previous 500ms stagger the second bubble began rising ~120ms after the first had finished its 380ms glide — long enough to avoid overlap (`LIFE-NODE-9`), not long enough for the first beat to register as its own moment, so a 2-beat turn read as one block arriving in pieces. **(2)** "Make sure options land after all events above them, with the same 1s delay, even if they arrive first." The options and the events resolve from independent calls (a live node ships its options in the SAME payload as its beats), so the options used to paint instantly while the beats were still gliding in underneath them. **ONE sequence, ONE owner.** `scheduleTurnArrival` drains the events, then SETTLES one further gap later; the options are a terminal step `release`d into it, and whichever side finishes last triggers the reveal — the queue if the options were already in hand, the options call if it outran the queue (`release` after settle runs immediately, so a slow generation is never charged a second gap). Two independent timers tuned to agree were rejected explicitly: they come apart the first time a generation runs slow, and a fixed delay measured from turn-start — the naive version — breaks for exactly the same reason. `createArrivalOwner` is the single place that knows a turn may have NO sequence (instant append, reduced motion, no beats), in which case the terminal step runs at once. Both option paths go through it: the bundled `options-ready` and, for the graded-demote path, `fetchOptions`'s `options-ready` AND its `options-failed` Continue ladder — a failure must not jump ahead of the events either. **ONE pacing number:** `nodeEngineGlide.BEAT_STAGGER_MS` is DEFINED as `EVENT_PACING_MS` rather than carrying its own literal — a second literal is exactly how the two drifted before, and the constraint `EVENT_PACING_MS ≥ GLIDE_ENTER_MS` is asserted, not assumed. **The pacing is VISUAL and non-blocking:** `scheduleTurnArrival` returns synchronously (it hands back `{ release, cancel }`, not a promise), so `commitNode`, the in-memory overlay and `handleSignals` all still run at arrival — the gaps defer only what is PAINTED. **While the options are held the phase stays `live-loading` / `options-loading`**, so the player keeps the loading placeholder rather than seeing an empty options area — the same reflow `LIFE-UI-12` removes. The two invariants are load-bearing for each other: the hold is only tolerable because the placeholder is the exact size of what replaces it. **A dropped event must never strand the player without options.** The sequence settles on EVERY termination except supersession — an empty queue, a hole in the queue, an `append` that throws (caught + logged, the queue carries on). Only `isStale` suppresses the release, and correctly so: a newer arrival owns the turn and brings its own options, so releasing there would paint the previous turn's chips over it. **Cancellation is part of the invariant:** the sequence outlives the call by ~1s per event plus one more, so every sequence registers a handle and `useNodeEngine` drains them all on unmount (`cancelPendingTurnArrivals`); `release` into a cancelled sequence is a no-op. A player who leaves mid-turn gets no append and no options reveal into an unmounted screen — and loses no data, because the write already happened (`LIFE-NODE-3`). A single-event turn is not held for an inter-event gap (it still gets the trailing one before its options). **Trigger is unchanged from `LIFE-NODE-9`:** pacing applies to arrivals that follow a player action (`appendMode: 'reset'`) — the opening node, question authoring and practice resolution keep their instant append and their instant options, and `prefers-reduced-motion` skips the whole sequence. |
| **LIFE-NODE-12** | **A beat enters on BOTH axes: it rises AND slides in from its own side** (`.life-beat-enter` in `globals.css`, `beatEnterOffsetX` in `nodeEngineGlide.ts`, applied in `DialogueArea.tsx`). Reported 2026-07-31: *"message bubbles in life sim only animate vertically not horizontally."* The entrance was `translateY(20px) → 0` and nothing else, so the player's line, the NPC's line and the narrator's card all arrived along the same vertical line and the side a bubble belongs to carried no motion at all. The keyframe now travels on both: `translate(var(--life-beat-enter-x, 0px), 20px) → translate(0, 0)`, over the SAME duration (`GLIDE_ENTER_MS`, 380ms) and the same `ease-out`. **The duration is unchanged, deliberately** — `LIFE-NODE-11` asserts `EVENT_PACING_MS ≥ GLIDE_ENTER_MS`, so a longer entrance would silently eat the inter-event calm the pacing pass just bought; the horizontal component is added INSIDE the existing window, never alongside it. **The direction is the bubble's own side, and it is derived from the speaker** (`beatEnterOffsetX`): a **user** beat is right-aligned, so it enters from the RIGHT (`+20px`); an **npc** beat is left-aligned, so it enters from the LEFT (`−20px`); a **narrator** beat is centered and has no side, so it stays at `0` and rises only. A beat sliding in from the wrong side reads as the wrong speaker — worse than no horizontal motion — so the mapping is asserted PER SPEAKER against the rendered DOM, not left implicit in the CSS. **One travel number, both axes** (`GLIDE_ENTER_TRAVEL_PX` = 20, mirrored from the CSS exactly as `GLIDE_ENTER_MS` already is): the entrance is a 45° diagonal rather than two independently tuned distances. It reaches the DOM as an inline custom property (`--life-beat-enter-x`) set from that constant, NOT as a Tailwind utility — `MSG-BUBBLE-8.1` applies unchanged here, a class built from a constant is invisible to the JIT, so the tests assert the RESOLVED property and never a class name. **`prefers-reduced-motion` drops BOTH axes** — the reduced-motion rule swaps the whole animation for `lifeBeatFade` (opacity only, no `transform` at all), so the horizontal component needs no separate guard and cannot be re-introduced by accident. **Shared with chat: the CONVENTION, not the constant** — see the design decision below. |

### Design decisions — the 2026-07-31 loading-block + event-pacing pass

Two owner reports, one turn's worth of polish. Both are recorded here because both had an obvious cheap fix that was rejected.

- **The loading block: shared measurements, not matched numbers (`LIFE-UI-12`).** The cheap fix was to add padding to the thinking bar until it looked the same height as the loaded state. That is what `LIFE-UI-10` effectively did for the grid, and it is why the bug came back: two states describing the same box in two places will drift the next time either is touched, and nothing in the test suite can catch it because jsdom does no layout, so there is no height to assert. The version that ships makes the drift impossible instead of unlikely — the frame row, the frame slot, the bar padding and the grid are one set of constants rendered by both states through one `OptionsFrame` component, and the tests assert (a) that the reserved height is DERIVED from the grid's own `grid-cols-2` / `gap-2` / chip-height, and (b) that the two states' rendered `className` strings are byte-identical. **What that does NOT prove:** that the resulting pixel heights match. Nothing available in the unit tier can prove that. What it proves is that the two states cannot be styled differently without a test failing, which is the reachable form of the requirement.

- **Skeleton count: 4, matching the typical turn rather than every turn.** The alternative was to remember the previous turn's chip count and render that many boxes. Rejected: it makes the loading state depend on history (so the first turn has no answer), and it optimises for the case that costs nothing. In a 2-column grid, 3 chips and 4 chips are both exactly two rows — and `LIFE-OPT-VARIETY` (2-3 options) plus the ✏️ chip on branch turns makes 3 and 4 the overwhelmingly common counts. The one case a fixed 4 gets wrong (a 2-option continuation = one row) is fixed on the OTHER side, by reserving the two rows on the real grid. The asymmetry is deliberate: a skeleton that is one row too tall is invisible; a skeleton that is one row too short is the reported bug.

- **The frame slot is a FIXED height, and the wait phrase inherits the title's typography.** Giving the phrase its own type scale inside a shared box would have worked for height (the box is fixed), but the two states would then differ visually at the swap for no reason — the owner's instruction was to put the loading indicator *in the title's slot*, and a slot with two different-looking occupants is a different design. Revisit if the uppercase eyebrow treatment reads badly on the longer wait phrases ("Seeing where that takes you…").

- **Event pacing: raise the ONE constant, don't add a second (`LIFE-NODE-11`).** `BEAT_STAGGER_MS` was already a named constant at 500ms, so the smallest possible change was to type `1000`. It is defined as `EVENT_PACING_MS` instead because the pacing is now a product requirement with a floor ("at least one second"), not an animation detail derived from the glide duration — the two happen to be compatible (1000 ≥ the 380ms glide), and the ordering constraint is asserted so they stay that way. Keeping them as two literals is how the 500 ended up being the only place the number lived.

- **Pacing stays VISUAL, and stays on the player-action path only.** The gap could have been implemented by awaiting between commits, which would have been simpler and wrong: it would hold the Firestore write, the local overlay the next prompt reads, and the signal application behind an animation. `scheduleTurnArrival` returns its handle synchronously and defers only what is painted. Extending the pacing to the `appendMode: 'append'` arrivals (opening node, question resolution) was considered and NOT done in this pass — those are not "a turn" in the sense the report meant, and the change would alter the opening's feel and touch the engine's timing tests. Flagged rather than done; revisit if the opening reads as a blob.

- **The options are a step in the sequence, not a second timer (`LIFE-NODE-11` part 2).** The cheap version is "delay the options by N ms from turn-start". It is wrong for a reason worth writing down: the events and the options come from independent resolutions, so the moment one generation runs slow the fixed delay expires mid-queue and the options land between two events — the exact symptom, back again, but now intermittent and blamed on the network. So the sequence owns both ends: it settles a gap after the LAST event and releases whatever terminal step is waiting; if the options have not resolved yet, THEY trigger the reveal when they land. The join is symmetric, which is what makes "even if they arrive first" a guarantee rather than a tuning.

- **Only supersession suppresses the release.** Everything else settles: no events, a hole in the queue, a render that throws. This asymmetry is deliberate and is the thing to preserve if the sequencer is ever rewritten — a player looking at a wurld with no options and no way forward is strictly worse than a player who briefly sees one beat fewer, so the failure path must bias toward revealing. Supersession is the one case where NOT releasing is correct, because a newer turn is already on its way with its own options.

- **Cancellation is in the invariant because the deferral got longer.** At 500ms × 2 beats the window where pending work could outlive the screen was ~1.3s; it is now ~3.3s (two events plus the trailing options gap). Cancellation is registered per-sequence and drained on unmount, and a `release` into a cancelled sequence is a no-op. Worth being precise about what this does and does not protect: no WRITE was ever at risk (the commit is synchronous on arrival, `LIFE-NODE-3`), and React no longer warns on a setState after unmount — what it removes is a genuine append (and now a genuine phase transition) into a dead tree, plus the timers behind them.

- **The hold and the placeholder are one change.** Holding the options for up to a second only works because `LIFE-UI-12` made the loading block the same size as the loaded block: without it the player would sit looking at a short placeholder and then watch the panel grow. Conversely the placeholder is on screen longer now, which is what makes its height matter more than it did. If either is reverted, revisit the other.

| **LIFE-NODE-13** | **An arriving beat OPENS ITS OWN ROW; the beats above ease upward rather than jumping.** Reported 2026-07-31: *"previous messages jump up then the new message glides in"* — and the report is precise about the order, which is what identifies the cause. `LIFE-NODE-9`/`-12` animate the bubble with a `transform` only, and a transform does not affect layout: the row reached full height the instant the beat mounted, so everything above it snapped up in one frame, and only THEN did the bubble glide into a gap that had already finished opening. Chat solved exactly this in `MSG-BUBBLE-8.2` and the mechanism ports without change, because the two surfaces have the same shape — a bottom-anchored column where a new row pushes the earlier ones up. Each beat is wrapped in a `.life-beat-grow` track (`DialogueArea.tsx`) whose `grid-template-rows` animates `0fr → 1fr`, **reusing chat's `bubbleEnterGrow` keyframes rather than declaring a second copy that could drift.** The two motions MUST stay on separate elements and separate durations — the track opens over 240ms while the bubble travels for the full `GLIDE_ENTER_MS` (380ms), so the beat is still arriving after the gap has stopped growing; equal durations pin the bubble's top to the gap's bottom edge the whole way, which reads as growing in place rather than arriving, and one element carrying both classes would collapse them onto a single timeline. The grid child needs `min-height: 0`, `min-width: 0` and `align-self: start` for the reasons already documented on `.bubble-enter-grow` (`MSG-BUBBLE-8.6`): without `align-self: start` the `0fr` track sizes the BUBBLE instead of the gap, and `min-width: auto` resolves to min-content and puts a floor under the bubble the plain flex child never had. **The travel distance is UNCHANGED at `LIFE-NODE-12`'s 20px diagonal** — this invariant is about the gap, not the glide, and lengthening the rise to chat's full bubble height would break the 45° diagonal `LIFE-NODE-12` settled on. `prefers-reduced-motion` disables the track animation, so the row simply appears at full height with the opacity-only fade. |
| **LIFE-NODE-14** | **A THROWN generate is RETRIED; it does not abandon the turn.** Reported 2026-08-03: *"I hit 'the story hit a snag' a lot. I think it's when the system decides to make a practice question from my point of view."* Both life-sim generators ran a retry ladder for CONTENT faults — invalid JSON, a schema miss, a wrong-shaped response, a leaked answer — and neither retried a **throw**. `generateGemini` rejecting (a dropped socket, a 429, a 5xx, the 45s abort) took a `catch` arm that `return`ed out of the loop, so one network hiccup went straight to `options-failed`: the snag screen, plus a Continue chip whose only job is to make the same call again. The retry existed; the transport never reached it. **The graded PLAN is not the fault.** Measured 2026-08-03 against real Gemini on a mid-scene fixture: 7/7 `gradedSayPlan`s built and generated cleanly, and 30/30 across the three step shapes on the graded critical path (the demote's options turn, the post-outcome live turn, the `say`-choice live turn). What the same runs DID show is transport: 2 of 8 first-pass calls died on a bare `fetch failed`. **Why the report points at graded turns:** a graded turn is the call-heaviest turn in the sim — the live turn that authored the option, then `life_graded_question`, then the post-outcome turn, where an ordinary pick makes one — so a per-call failure rate concentrates exactly where the player noticed it. `generateWithTransportRetry` (`lib/life/generateWithRetry.ts`) wraps a single content attempt in both generators. **The two budgets are SEPARATE, deliberately:** a dropped socket says nothing about the model's output, so charging it to `MAX_ATTEMPTS` would let one blip disarm the schema ladder for the rest of the turn. **Bounded at 2 retries** (`TRANSPORT_BACKOFF_MS` = [400, 1200], so the array length IS the retry count and the added latency is legible at ≤1.6s) — a ladder long enough to outlast a real outage reads to the player as a hang. **Only failures that can plausibly clear on their own are retried** (`isTransientGenError`): offline, timeout, rate-limited, a bare `fetch failed` with no `AppError` code, and the `pipeline/failed` cases whose message carries a 408/429/5xx or the abort. Auth, authorisation and other 4xx are NOT — they reproduce exactly, and retrying spends the player's wait to land in the same place. |
| **LIFE-NODE-15** | **A turn that fails is VISIBLE in the debug overlay — including a failure it recovered from.** Reported 2026-08-03: *"Make sure events logged to the debug window in life sim log even if they fail, and log the reason they failed even if they retry."* The step generator reported every rejection through `log.warn` ONLY (`life.node.gen-failed` / `-schema-failed` / `-validator-issues` / `-beat-budget-clamped` / `-graded-direction-repaired` / `-graded-direction-unrepairable`), and two of its rejections — invalid JSON and a wrong-shaped response for the turn — reported NOTHING at all. Those never reached the life debug ring buffer (`lib/life/debugLog.ts`), so a turn that snagged showed the player a Continue chip and showed the overlay an empty page, while the sibling generator (`runGradedQuestionGenerate`, `LIFE-EX-GEN-LOG`) had logged its rejections since v2.1. The buffer is the ONLY trace that survives a route change, so "check the console" is not a substitute. **(a) Every rejection inside `generateStepOptions` pushes to the buffer via `lifeFailed('STEP', …)`** — the throw that abandons the turn, each attempt rejected for invalid JSON / schema / wrong turn shape, an unrepairable graded direction (`LIFE-SPEECH-1`), and a canon rejection that spends a retry (`LIFE-CANON-2`). Each payload carries the **attempt index**, the **structured issues**, the **retry hint that was actually sent**, and a **bounded (≤600 char) sample of the raw response** — enough to diagnose without re-deriving anything from raw JSON. **(b) Exhaustion emits ONE terminal entry naming the LAST reason** (`step exhausted retries → snag`, with `lastReason`), mirroring `LIFE-EX-GEN-9`'s `exhausted-retries: <last violation>`: the final line of the buffer answers "why did this turn snag" without expanding every attempt above it. **(c) A RECOVERED fault is warn-level but NOT `-FAILED`.** `lifeWarn(channel, …)` pushes `level:'warn'` under the plain `LIFE/<channel>` tag, so the overlay renders it amber (it is a problem) without claiming the turn died. It carries the two in-place repairs — the `LIFE-LANG-13.1` beat clamp and the `LIFE-SPEECH-1` direction repair — plus a `LIFE-NODE-14` transport retry. Routing these through `lifeFailed` was rejected: the beat clamp fires on the ORDINARY `say` turn, so a `-FAILED` badge on every healthy speech turn would bury the real failures the overlay exists to surface. **(d) A transport retry is logged at the moment it is DECIDED, not at the end**, so an attempt that eventually SUCCEEDS still leaves its reason (`category`, `attempt`, `AppError` code, backoff) in the buffer — the "log the reason they failed even if they retry" half of the report, and the only trace a recovered blip ever produces. **(e) Additive.** Every existing `log.warn` stays, so the browser console is unchanged. |
| **LIFE-NODE-16** | **A response with ONE obvious correct reading is REPAIRED, not rejected.** MEASURED 2026-08-04 by replaying a four-world run's 184 `life_step` responses through the schema: **13 (7%) failed the strict parse on the first attempt**, and only 2 exhausted the retry ladder and became visible failures — so the other 11 were an invisible tax of a whole ~21k-token regeneration each, on the most expensive call in the app. One of the two visible ones landed on turn 0 of a scene and cost the run an ENTIRE eight-turn scene. `repairStepResponse` runs on the raw parsed JSON at the single `safeParse` seam in `generateStepOptions`, before the strict schema sees it. **Four repairs, each with exactly one defensible reading:** **(a) `scene.phaseTransition: null` is treated as ABSENT.** This was never a model error — `null` is the natural way to say "no phase transition" and the field is `.optional()`, so a correct response was throwing away a correct turn. Fixed at the schema (`.nullable()`) rather than in the repair pass, because the field means the same thing either way. Scanning every null path the run produced showed this was the ONLY optional the model nulls that the schema refused — the rest (`npcId`, `speakerName`, `targetId`, `transportMode`) were already nullable. **(b) A `continuation` carrying 2-3 well-formed distinct-type options IS a branch** — relabel `kind`, don't reject. The model wrote a real choice and mislabelled the container; `branch` accepts 0-2 beats and a continuation carries 1-2, so the relabel is always shape-valid. Rejecting threw away three authored options to punish one wrong word. **(c) A `continuation` whose single option's id isn't `"continue"` has its id FORCED.** The id is a protocol token, not content — the player sees the option's text either way. **(d) A malformed `resolution` is DROPPED, not fatal.** This one is not a new decision: `LIFE-EX-PLAN-5` already specified the degradation in prose *("a resolution that arrives malformed is dropped rather than failing the response … `feedbackContinue` falls back to a live turn")* and the code added a `ctx` issue instead, failing the whole turn. The comment described behaviour that was never implemented. **What is NOT repaired: a branch with two options of the same `actionType`.** There is no correct reading — you cannot invent the missing third kind, and relabelling one would lie to the grid rotation that reads it. `LIFE-GRID-1.1`'s own note sets the threshold at which the rule rather than the model is wrong (~15%); measured here at 2.2%, so the rule stands and those spend a retry as designed. |
| **LIFE-NODE-16e** | **`scene.resolved: null` is NULLABLE, for the same reason `phaseTransition` is (LIFE-NODE-16a).** `null` is the natural way to write "the scene did not resolve this turn", the field already means that when absent, and the strict parse charged a full ~21k-token regeneration for the choice between the two spellings. Observed once in the 2026-08-05 four-world run. LIFE-NODE-16a's scan concluded `phaseTransition` was the ONLY optional the model nulls that the schema refused — that was true of the run it scanned and is no longer true, so **the class is not closed**: future optional OBJECT fields on `ZStepSceneSignal` should be `nullish()` from the start rather than waiting to cost a turn to discover it. |
| **LIFE-NODE-19** | **A response followed by one stray closing brace is parsed, not thrown away.** Observed TWICE in a single four-world run with an identical signature: a complete, well-formed object followed by one extra `}`. The step path's `safeParseJson` did exactly one repair — strip code fences — so each cost the whole `life_step` call plus a ~21k-token retry, for a typo. `parseUpToBalance` walks the text tracking brace depth (string- and escape-aware) and parses the prefix that ends where depth first returns to zero. **Trailing EXCESS closers only.** A TRUNCATED tail (more openers than closers) is deliberately NOT repaired — the content is genuinely missing and inventing it is a guess, which is worse than the retry. That is exactly the line `LIFE-NODE-16` draws between repair and regenerate. |
| **LIFE-EX-PLAN-6** | **When the plan's `testTarget` is absent from its `sentenceContext`, the SENTENCE wins and the target check is dropped.** This was previously a violation, and the violation was **UNWINNABLE**. The defect belongs to the PLAN, authored by `life_step` one call earlier; `runGradedQuestionGenerate` re-sends `input.plan` byte-identical on every attempt, so the generator was being asked to fix something it does not own — while the same prompt told it *"the 'correct' chip is the testTarget itself"*. Both attempts failed identically and the question was **DROPPED**. Measured 7 times across 47 stored transcripts. The shape of it is German: `testTarget "können"` against `"Ich kann das Logbuch einfach nicht finden."` — the model planned the LEMMA and wrote the INFLECTED form, which is not an error in any sense a learner would recognise. In one world it cost 2 of 9 planned questions, invisibly: the player saw ordinary narrative, no snag, just less teaching. The sentence is what the player is about to SAY, so it is authoritative; the target is the model's label for a word inside it. When they disagree, test the word that is actually there — the correct chip must appear in `sentenceContext`, which is the property the generator actually needs and, unlike the target check, one it can satisfy. No morphology is attempted (no stemming, no edit distance): the generator already picks the blank from the sentence and only had to be ALLOWED to. **The general lesson: a retry hint addressed to an actor who cannot act on it is not a retry, it is a guaranteed loss.** |
| **LIFE-EX-PLAN-7** | **A recycled fill-blank target DEMOTES the question, at the cost of zero calls.** The prompt states the rule as hard — *"FRESH TARGETS ONLY (HARD): it MUST NOT appear in `<recentlyPracticed>`. Never recycle."* — `<recentlyPracticed>` is rendered on every turn, and **nothing anywhere checked it**. Measured: one world planned `escaleras` three times, with the word VISIBLY in the block on the second and third; the next run recycled two more. A rule stated in a prompt and checked nowhere is a suggestion. The consequence is a demote rather than a retry for the same reason as `LIFE-EX-PLAN-6`: the plan is re-sent unchanged, so retrying is the same unwinnable loop. Demoting costs the turn its question — the node still renders and the cadence counter picks it up next turn — and costs zero extra calls, where recycling costs a question the player has already answered. |

| **LIFE-INTENT-1** | **What the player just did HAPPENED, and the next beat may not be written as though it did not.** Reported as the owner's first-and-foremost concern. The rule as stated in `prompts/step.ts`: the response must fail the test *"could this have been written if the player had done something ELSE?"*; **free text OUTRANKS the subject** (a pick that takes the scene somewhere the scene did not plan is absorbed — a little pushback is fine, but the pushback IS the consequence, not a way of putting the player back where they were); and **the options after must follow from what happened**, not from what the scene was about. `LIFE-SCENE-25`'s "every beat serves the subject" carries the matching exception, and `optionGrid.ts` states the precedence again at the point the rotation is chosen, because a rule ~500 lines up in the cached prefix lost to a `<requiredAction>` in the volatile tail. **MEASURED RESULT — it worked, and it stopped exactly halfway.** Across 19 scenes of a four-world benchmark the judge said the same thing 19 times in different words: *"the choices are acknowledged by the narrative, but the scene progresses along a fixed path."* Absorption of an unexpected free-text action went to 3/3. The floor moved from IGNORED to ACKNOWLEDGED, and the mean stayed at **1.29-1.5 of 4**. That is the ceiling of what a prose instruction can buy: the model can honour the pick in the sentence while the scene's destination is untouched, because the destination was never the pick's to move. `LIFE-INTENT-2` is the structural half. |
| **LIFE-INTENT-2** | **The scene carries an OPEN QUESTION and a LEANING, and the leaning is a state a pick can move.** The diagnosis LIFE-INTENT-1's measurement produced: a scene has a `subject`, the subject is a DESTINATION, and every turn's job is to arrive at it — so a pick can colour the journey and never change where it goes, because there is nowhere else to go. **(a) `sceneOpen` emits `openQuestion`** — the unsettled thing, present tense, naming no outcome ("will Bruno give up the machinery?"). **(b) The scene carries `leaning`** — one clause on where that question sits RIGHT NOW, seeded by `sceneOpen` and rewritten by the step model every live turn. **(c) The step prompt renders the question and the CURRENT leaning**, and the response must return `scene.leaning` plus `scene.leaningMoved`. **(d) The stall counter is the mechanism.** A leaning may legitimately repeat — an NPC walks in, the player observes — so repeating is allowed but must be DECLARED (`leaningMoved: false`), and consecutive declines are counted in code. At 2 the prompt says so and requires the next option set to contain one that can actually move it. This is deliberately the shape of `LIFE-EX-CADENCE-5`, which is the last thing in this codebase that worked: **doing nothing is what increments the counter**, so "satisfy it by doing nothing" is not available. **CONTRADICTION CHECKED, AND THE FIRST DESIGN WAS DROPPED.** The obvious mechanism is an open question plus 2-3 candidate answers a pick selects between. `LIFE-ARCH-3` bans `candidateEndings` **by name** — measured as a monotonic funnel that only ever narrowed, 3 → 2 → 1, until the scene was about nothing. An enumerated answer set is that field with a new name, so it is not built. What LIFE-ARCH-3 names as the correct replacement is *"what people want, what is in motion, what happens if nobody acts"* — and a leaning is precisely "what is in motion": it describes the PRESENT, stores no outcome, and cannot narrow because there is no set to remove from. **The open question is NOT an exit condition** (that would be `completionTriggers`, banned in the same clause): `LIFE-SCENE-END-1` still ends the scene on material change, and a scene may end with its question unanswered — which is itself a real outcome the benchmark already produces ("your attempt was met with prideful silence"). |
| **LIFE-INTENT-3** | **A leaning is a POSITION on the scene's question. A restated question is not one, and does not count as a move.** MEASURED on the first run where `LIFE-INTENT-2` was actually switched on: the engine reported 42-52% of turns moving the scene while the judge still scored INTENT at 1.56, and the reason was visible the moment the leanings were read next to the questions they answered. **36% of leanings (48 of 134) were themselves questions** — *"whether Jacinto successfully uses the distraction"*, *"whether the Maestra believes Luz"*, *"whether Inés can prove her precision"* — and **a different question almost every turn**. They accounted for **37% of all credited moves**. The model was not stating where the answer stood; it was REPLACING the question, which is the exact inverse of the mechanism: instead of a fixed question whose answer moves, a moving question with no answer. `leaningMoved` was trivially true because the text genuinely differed every turn. **The check is on FORM, not meaning** — a clause opening with "whether" or "if", or ending in a question mark, is grammatically interrogative whatever it is about. That distinction is load-bearing: three earlier attempts to score narrative properties by substring (OPT-LEAK, CLICHE, and a third) all failed and were moved to the judge, because they were asking a lexical test to hold an opinion about MEANING. "Is this sentence a question" is not an opinion. Measured across the same 134: a deliberately over-broad detector (adding `will`/`can`/`has`/`is`/…) and the narrow one flag the IDENTICAL 48, so the narrow rule costs nothing in recall and cannot misfire on a legitimate position like *"will refuse unless you pay first"* — which is a perfectly good answer and must keep counting. **A restated question does not overwrite the stored leaning and does not clear the stall**, so writing one INCREMENTS the counter: getting it wrong is treated exactly like doing nothing, which is the only shape this codebase has found that the do-nothing escape does not survive. |

| **LIFE-SCENE-32** | **A resolving scene ALWAYS closes, and the close starts BEFORE the celebration screen opens.** Reported: *"the scene summary should start being written as soon as the last play is made, not when the modal opens."* Investigating it found a defect underneath the latency one. `runSceneClose` is what WRITES the history line, and `LIFE-SCENE-31` renders that line as the scene-complete screen's summary — but the call was gated on `plan.shouldReplan`, which is a 5-turn tick plus the two pivot signals. **A scene that resolved on any turn where the tick had not come round produced no summary at all**, and the completion screen pulsed its placeholder for ever. The tick governs when a RUNNING scene refreshes its state; it has no business deciding whether an ENDING one gets recorded, so the condition is now `shouldReplan || resolvingNow`. **The ordering half:** `onSceneResolved` used to fire before the close was even started, so the whole round-trip was spent with a placeholder on screen. `resolvingNow` is computed at the top of `applyTurnSignals`, the close is kicked off from it, and the screen opens LAST — the snapshot it renders was ready before any of this ran, so nothing is delayed by the reorder. The earliest possible moment is the one used: the resolution is not knowable until the step response carrying it lands, and this is that instant. |
| **LIFE-DAY-2.1** | **`closeSceneFlow` RETURNS the arc it just wrote, and the chapter archive uses it.** Found 2026-08-11 while re-pointing `LIFE-EX-10`, and it is a real defect rather than a test problem: the close writes the finished day into `currentArc.completedSceneMemories`, then `useChapterCompletion` handed `archiveActiveArc` its own `opts.world` — the render-time snapshot, captured before that write. So **every chapter recap was one day short, and a chapter of a single scene archived an EMPTY day list** — which is exactly the content `LIFE-SCENE-31` promoted to be that card's LEAD. The card was built to open with the days and could not show the last one. Fixed by returning the updated arc from the close (it is already in hand there, so no re-read and no listener round-trip) and spreading it over the world passed to the archive. **Why it hid:** the only spec that would have caught it asserted `-Outcome`, an element `LIFE-SCENE-31` had deleted, so it failed for a stale-selector reason and was never read past. Its replacement assertion then went unverified for a further day because the spec kept bailing earlier in the file. Three separate failures had to be cleared before the product bug underneath was visible at all. |
| **LIFE-SCENE-31.1** | **The summary slot must reach a TERMINAL STATE — the close reports exactly once, on every path, and a card that will never be filled says so.** Reported: *"the what happened section in the scene summary needs to be started as soon as we know it's the scene summary, ie while we're doing card matching. At the moment it just hangs indefinitely."* **The timing half of that was already true and is not what was broken** — `LIFE-SCENE-32` starts the close in the same synchronous block that arms the card-match review, so it already has the whole review to land in, and there is no earlier instant available (the resolution is not knowable until the step response carrying it lands). What was broken is that the close had **three silent dead ends** against a card that renders `summary === null` as a pulsing placeholder with no terminal state, so any of them shimmered for ever: `runSceneClose` returning `failed`; returning ok with an empty history line; anything in the block throwing (`updateLifeWorld`, `updateActiveScene`). Each one `return`ed without telling the screen. **The fix is the state, not the speed.** `onSceneSummary` now fires **exactly once per resolve out of a `finally`**, and `null` means *"we tried and there is no record"* — a terminal state, distinct from *"not back yet"*, which is the ABSENCE of the call. `LocalState.sceneSummaryDone` carries that distinction to `SceneCompleteScreen`, which renders three states rather than two: the record, `life.sceneComplete.summaryUnavailable` when done-with-nothing, the shimmer otherwise. **A shimmer is a promise that something is coming; when nothing is, saying so plainly is the only honest render.** Two supporting decisions: (a) **the resolution beat is the fallback** — `outcomeLineNative || outcomeLine` is at least TRUE about how the scene ended, so a failed close costs the world's compaction, not the player's summary; (b) **a 25s timeout** on the close, because `runSceneClose`'s transport retry bounds each attempt and nothing bounded the total — a promise that never settles is indistinguishable to the player from the failures the `finally` catches. 25s is picked against what it competes with: the card-match review takes a player the better part of a minute, so a close still in flight at 25s has already lost the race it was started early to win. **`landed` is assigned AFTER the world write, not at the response**, so `LIFE-SCENE-31`'s rule that the card and the world cannot disagree survives a failed write. The close moved to `nodeEngineSceneClose.ts` in the same change — one self-contained job with one caller, and `nodeEngineActions.ts` was 20 lines over the 600-LOC cap. |
| **LIFE-SCENE-33** | **The scene boundary is a CONTAINER in `<recentEvents>`, not one more row in it.** The block is a flat chronological list; a scene change was marked by a single `<event kind="scene_started">` carrying the same weight as a beat, with nothing around it saying the events above were over. On the turn a scene OPENS, those events are the only ones in the window — so the loudest signal in the block is the last thing that happened somewhere the player has left. Observed (`.playthroughs/2026-08-05T09-05-04-618Z-Ines`, scene 2): the scene declares `where = "La Biblioteca de los Cien Pasos"` and opens on *"Sweat runs down your forehead while the stone vibrates under the weight of the tide"* — scene 1's sea grotto, continued straight through the boundary. Note what a lexical check makes of that: the wording is entirely fresh, so n-gram overlap with the previous scene scored it **0**, and across 132 stored scene-openers NONE reached 15% overlap. The carry-over is semantic, which is why the fix is structural and not another line of prose asking for freshness. `renderRecentEvents` now partitions the window at the active scene's own `scene_started`: everything before it goes inside `<priorScene status="over">` with the instruction attached to the data (*finished; here for continuity; NOT where the player is, NOT who they are with, NOT the conversation they are in*), everything after inside `<currentScene at="…">`. When `<currentScene>` is EMPTY — the exact failing turn — it says so: the beat being written now is the scene's FIRST, so open it. **Scope**: the partition fires while that boundary is still inside the render window AND something precedes it; once the new scene fills the window the block reverts byte-for-byte to the flat list. **Corrected 2026-08-05 after measuring**: the original note here claimed this was "a handful of turns". It is not — a turn produces 1-2 events against a 20-event window, so the boundary stays visible for most of a scene and the partition rendered on **130 of 226** `life_step` calls (58%) in the next full run. That is harmless (the block is two lines of note text in the volatile tail, so no prefix-cache cost) but the original claim was wrong and the reasoning it rested on — "the common mid-scene turn is unchanged" — did not hold. |
| **LIFE-SCENE-34** | **~~The dominant failure class~~ — CORRECTED 2026-08-05. "Scene never resolved" was mostly an artefact of where the HARNESS ceiling sat, not scenes running away.** The original row read the 30-of-43 "never resolved — cut at the 14-turn ceiling" failures as a product defect. Re-run with the ceiling at **30** instead of 14: **six of six scenes resolved on their own at 11-14 turns**, zero failures. Scenes naturally land at 11-14; the rig stopped at 14; a scene that would have resolved at 15 was recorded as a failure. **The check that settles a question like this — remove the instrument's limit and see what the system does — cost one run and should have come first.** **What survives**: scenes 0-2 are cut more often than scenes 3+ (25% vs 8% on balanced samples), so early scenes DO run longer. And the chapter-clock hypothesis is still rejected (20% resolve with `<chapterPace>` present, 30% without). What does NOT survive is the framing that scenes loop or never end. **The app has no ceiling at all** — `signal?.resolved` from the model is the only thing that ends a scene client-side. That remains true and is fine: the owner's position is that length is not the defect, LOOPING is (*"if the story enter a loop where the user was being asked to collect signatures but never able to do so"*), and a scene that runs to 20 with a genuine unresolved reason is a pass. Reading the longest scenes confirmed movement every turn — new places, new people, new information — not circling. **Subject drift is NOT a defect** (owner: *"I'm actually fine with the scene resolving a different question than was initially set. That's great that it's moving with the story."*). Roughly half of scenes resolve on a subject other than the one declared; that is the story following the player, and it is wanted. |
| **LIFE-ARC-PACE-5** | **`DAY_PACE_RULE` deleted — a scene is no longer a day, and the rule was forbidding the thing we want.** It was live in the step SI and said: *"one scene IS one day (LIFE-DAY-1), and a day should run roughly 6-9 player turns — plan the day's shape from the start, so its beats read as morning → afternoon → evening … a day that resolves while the narration is still describing mid-morning reads as broken."* Three faults. (a) A scene is no longer a day, so it asked for a whole day's arc inside a much smaller unit — pressure to keep going and to fill the time with whatever came to hand. (b) The mid-morning clause **explicitly forbids early resolution**, which is the opposite of what `<scenePace>` is trying to achieve three blocks later. (c) It advertised a `<dayPace>` block that **has never existed** — the renderer emits `<scenePace>`. **Why deleting it does not cost the pacing** (the owner's question on approving it): the turn window was stated TWICE and the versions disagreed — this rule said 6-9, `WHAT A SCENE IS` (LIFE-SCENE-25) says 6-12, and 6/9/12 are the thresholds `renderScenePace` actually escalates on. The survivor is the correct one. Three pressure sources remain and are pinned by `scenePacePressure.test.ts`: that rule, the `<scenePace>` block escalating from turn 6, and `sceneProgress` 0-100 every turn. **Measured, same two seeds and the same 30-turn ceiling before and after**: mean scene length **12.7 → 12.0** (Alice) and **13.3 → 11.7** (Ines); longest scene 15. Directionally right in both worlds and consistent with the prediction that removing a ban on early resolution tightens rather than loosens — but **six scenes across two single runs is not proof**, and playthrough variance between different stories is much larger than this gap. The claim that holds is the negative one: removal did not produce runaway scenes. **Not replaced with a scene-scoped "let time advance" clause.** Considered and declined — a scene may now span twenty minutes of story time, so instructing time to move across it recreates the same problem smaller. If scenes start reading as frozen in time, add that back, scoped to the scene and with no resolution ban attached. |

| **LIFE-BENCH-1** | **The benchmark's NOISE FLOOR, measured — and most single-run deltas are under it.** Two runs of BYTE-IDENTICAL code, judged at temperature 0, differ by **0.016 composite**; per dimension the spread is **CLICHE-NAME 1.00; Q-CADENCE / SUBJECT-SPREAD / COHERENCE / PACING 0.50; INTENT / OPT-FREEDOM / CLICHE / CAST-DEPTH / QUESTION-VALUE / INTENT-MOVED 0.25; everything else ≤0.13**. Fixing the judge's temperature (`judge.ts`) removed the INSTRUMENT's noise; this is the remaining source and it is not removable, because each run generates a DIFFERENT STORY and the four worlds are a sample of four. **A claimed effect smaller than its dimension's figure above is not an effect.** Applying that retroactively: the three `<sceneQuestion>` sizings (1400 / 640 / 508 chars) span 0.029 composite against a 0.016 floor and differ on INTENT by ≤0.25 against a 0.25 floor — **there is no measurable difference between them**, so the smallest that preserves the mechanism is the right one. What DOES clear the floor, by a wide margin: the scene-ceiling fix (composite **+0.368**, 23× the floor) and the ablation's `INTENT-MOVED` (**4.00 with the block vs 1.25 without**, 11× its floor) — the block genuinely drives the leaning mechanism even though its effect on the judged story dimensions cannot be resolved at n=4. **Planning consequence:** at four worlds this suite can only detect INTENT effects of roughly half a band. Detecting a 0.1 effect needs on the order of 25 worlds, so a change whose predicted effect is small should not be evaluated by adding another four-world run — it should be evaluated by a deterministic counter (movement rate, failure count, rejection count), which is why those exist alongside the judge. Re-derive with two runs of unchanged code whenever the mark scheme or world set changes. |
| **LIFE-BENCH-2** | **A low score is a claim about the MARK SCHEME as much as about the product, and INTENT's bands contradicted their own procedure.** Challenged by the product owner on the reported INTENT/OPT-FREEDOM scores: *"if I say pick up the bread, normally the next event is you pick up the bread… for the immediate one I'm not sure I agree."* Reading the transcripts as the judge confirmed it.
