Choosing *Pause for now* — or any failure of that one call — left the world **permanently arc-less and still playable**, a dead state with no recovery path: `sceneGenerate` gets an empty `<worldArc>`, the idle `<scene>` block has no through-line to serve (LIFE-WORLD-ARC-4), and `archiveActiveArc` early-returns on `!active` so the chapter can *never* complete again. Observed on a real world (`w-1785453145426-8w0ffo`, 2026-07-31): `completedArcCount: 1`, `currentArc: null`, `storyLog.turns: 37`, **one** hook offered across 110 events, and the only chapter that ever ended did so because the player typed "and the chapter" into free-text. **The world-open path therefore regenerates a missing arc**: `useArcSelfHeal` fires `runWorldArcGenerate({ chapterNumber: completedArcCount + 1 })` once per mount when `currentArc` is absent, non-blocking and failure-tolerant, mirroring LIFE-WORLD-ARC-3's chapter-1 behaviour. It is idempotent (a ref latch plus the arc landing removes the precondition) and it repairs pre-existing stranded worlds on next open — no migration. **The next chapter number is `completedArcCount + 1`, NOT a remembered value:** the archived arc is the only record of what was completed, and the count is the scalar the clean-break data model keeps for exactly this (LIFE-WORLD-ARC-1). |
| **LIFE-WORLD-ARC-10** | **`arcStage: 'concluding'` is the ONLY thing that ends a chapter, and the model must be told what it is concluding.** The three signals that make it actionable all render now: `<chapterPace>` (how far in, LIFE-ARC-PACE-1), `<hookPressure>` (how long without a scene, LIFE-ARC-PACE-3), and the chapter's own through-line in the idle `<scene>` block (LIFE-WORLD-ARC-4). Before all three, the instruction "bring the through-line home" named a through-line the prompt did not contain — the same unobservable-quantity defect as LIFE-EX-CADENCE-1. |
| **LIFE-WORLD-ARC-8** | The `WorldArcCompleteScreen` shows: chapter number + premise, the model's outcomeLine, aggregated stats across the chapter (scenes completed, practice questions, accuracy), and the recurring-NPC names. Compact celebration of the through-line before the Continue/Pause choice. |

---

## 10.8 Recurring NPCs across scenes — `LIFE-NPC-RECUR-*`

NPCs the player connects with deeply survive past their scene. The model promotes them at scene close; future scenes can lean on them as recurring characters. As the recurring roster grows, the model also retires old NPCs to keep the world from accumulating untracked baggage.

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| **LIFE-NPC-RECUR-1** | `ZLifeNpc.scope` is `'scene' \| 'world' \| 'transient'`. `'scene'` (default at promotion time) = bound to one scene. `'world'` = recurring across scenes within the world. `'transient'` = retiring; dropped from the roster on the next turn. |
| **LIFE-NPC-RECUR-2** | `scene.resolved.promotedNpcIds[]` lists ids the model promotes from scene-scope to world-scope on close. `closeSceneFlow` runs `promoteNpcToWorld(uid, worldId, npcId)` for each → sets `scope: 'world'`, clears `sceneId`. The id is also appended to the active `worldArc.recurringNpcIds[]`. |
| **LIFE-NPC-RECUR-3** | `scene.resolved.retiredNpcIds[]` lists world-scope NPC ids the model retires (moved away, died, fell out of touch, lost interest). `closeSceneFlow` runs `retireNpcToTransient(uid, worldId, npcId)` for each → sets `scope: 'transient'`. The id is removed from the active arc's `recurringNpcIds[]`. |
| **LIFE-NPC-RECUR-4** | The roster filter in `runLifeStep` is `(scope !== 'transient') ∧ (sceneId == null ∨ sceneId == activeSceneId)`. Transient NPCs are excluded from `<npcRoster>`; the model never sees them again. Their doc rows remain (cheap; cleanup is a future job). |
| **LIFE-NPC-RECUR-5** | Soft cap of 8 recurring NPCs per arc. The SI nudges the model to FAVOUR retirement on scene resolves once `arc.recurringNpcIds.length >= 8`. The list is UNCAPPED in storage — the cap is purely a model-side heuristic. Eviction is the model's call, not the client's. |
| **LIFE-NPC-RECUR-6** | The `<npcRoster>` block in the lifeStep prompt now carries a `scope` attribute on each row (`world` / `scene`) so the model can distinguish recurring NPCs from arc-bound ones at a glance. The active arc's `recurringNpcIds[]` is also included as a separate `<recurringNpcs>` block to highlight which world NPCs the model should lean on this scene. |

---

## 10.9 Chapter vocab recap — `LIFE-VOCAB-RECAP-*`

The scene / chapter completion screens end with a "Words you practised" list — a lightweight review of the vocabulary the player just met, derived entirely from the event log. No LLM call, no extra Firestore read: the list is a pure function of the already-loaded `useLifeEvents` window.

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| **LIFE-VOCAB-RECAP-1** | The list is derived CLIENT-SIDE by `derivePractisedWords(events, sinceSeconds)` (`src/lib/life/vocabRecap.ts`) — zero LLM calls, zero additional Firestore reads. Input is the already-loaded `useLifeEvents` window (last 40 events, chronological oldest → newest). Rules: (a) `correction` events with `createdAt.seconds >= sinceSeconds` contribute `payload.correctAnswer` (trimmed; empty/missing → skipped) with `correct = payload.correct === true`; (b) `flashcard_added` events in the same window contribute `payload.practice` (trimmed; empty/missing → skipped) with `translation = payload.native` (trimmed, `null` when empty); (c) entries dedupe case-insensitively on the trimmed text — the first occurrence keeps its position and casing, a later `correction` overwrites `correct` (latest attempt wins), a later `flashcard_added` fills a missing `translation`; (d) every other event kind is ignored; (e) an event with no `createdAt` counts as seconds 0 (excluded by any positive window start). |
| **LIFE-VOCAB-RECAP-2** | Both completion screens render the list through the shared `VocabRecapList` (`src/screens/LifeScreen/VocabRecapList.tsx`) and only when it is non-empty (empty → the section renders nothing, not an empty header). **Both lists derive REACTIVELY at render time** in the completion-overlay layer (`LifeCompletionOverlays`) from the live `useLifeEvents` window: `SceneCompleteScreen` over the scene window `snapshot.windowStartSec (= acceptedAt) → now`; `WorldArcCompleteScreen` over the chapter window `arc.startedAt → now`. The scene snapshot carries only the window START (`SceneCompletionSnapshot.windowStartSec`), NOT a frozen word list: a resolve-time snapshot of `p.events` raced the Firestore listener — admin-seeded / just-written events not yet delivered to `p.events` were permanently dropped from the frozen list — so deriving reactively lets a re-render pick them up (the E2E `LIFE-VOCAB-RECAP-2` flake). Display caps at 12 chips; overflow renders a single "+N more" chip. A missing window start degrades to seconds 0 — "everything in the loaded window" — never a crash. Both windows are bounded by the loaded 40-event window, same as the completion stats (see §20 design decision). |
| **LIFE-VOCAB-RECAP-3** | **Chapter KEY WORDS** (v2.1). The `WorldArcCompleteScreen` shows a SECOND vocab section — "Key words this chapter" (`deriveKeyWords`, `LifeScreen-KeyWords` testid) — distinct from "Words you practised" (`LIFE-VOCAB-RECAP-2`, tested + saved words). Key words are the STORY's own recurring vocabulary: harvested from the `beat` events' `complexWords`/`wordTranslations` in the chapter window (`arc.startedAt → now`), deduped case-insensitively, FREQUENCY-RANKED (a word the story kept returning to ranks first), capped at 12. Zero LLM, zero extra reads. Renders through the same `VocabRecapList` (now parameterised with an optional `title` + `testid`); empty → nothing. |

---

## 11. Transport loop — `LIFE-TRANS-*`

Mask scene-gen latency by showing a journey screen while the next scene renders.

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| **LIFE-TRANS-1** | Triggered whenever `LIFE-STEP-3` returns a `backdropIntent` indicating a scene change. The orchestrator: (a) writes a `decision` event capturing the transport mode, (b) flips the BackdropStage to the transport loop component, (c) starts the scene render if not cached. |
| **LIFE-TRANS-2** | Transport modes come from a fixed enum: `walk | metro | car | bus | train | bike | plane | boat | teleport | magic-portal`. The step response includes the chosen mode (the model picks from the enum). Each mode has a hand-drawn loop animation that's part of the fallback asset library — ZERO render cost, always available. |
| **LIFE-TRANS-3** | The transport loop UI shows: the mode icon + animation centred, a "Travelling to **{destination}**…" label in practice + native (per `MSG-VIEW-*`), and a small progress shimmer underneath. The label uses the destination's `locationLabel` from `backdropIntent`. |
| **LIFE-TRANS-4** | When BOTH the new scene and the first beat on that scene are ready, the orchestrator flips: transport loop crossfades out, the new scene fades in, the first beat bubble slides in. The label updates from "Travelling to…" to "Arrived at {destination}" briefly (1.5s) before fading to the standard location ribbon. |
| **LIFE-TRANS-5** | The transport loop is interruptible — if the user backgrounds the app mid-transit, on resume the loop continues from where it stopped (or skips straight to "Arrived" if the scene is now ready). The transport mode is never re-decided. |
| **LIFE-TRANS-6** | Transit time is purely cosmetic — minimum display time 1.5s even if cache-hit (so the journey feels intentional), maximum display time 30s (after which a "still travelling…" extension hint can render). |

---

## 12. Durable memory — `LIFE-MEM-*` (compaction retired)

**`LIFE-COMPACT-1..6` are RETIRED (WS1-C, 2026-07).** The `life_compact` call, `runCompaction.ts`, `compactionPrompts.ts`, `compactionSchema.ts`, and the budget trigger in `generateStepOptions` were all DELETED. The lossy, wholesale-rewritten `rollingSummary` — a third parallel "what happened" store maintained by a blocking extra Gemini round-trip on the critical path — was the redundant tier. The memory hierarchy is now **two durable tiers by fidelity**: the append-only `storyLog` spine (long range) + the verbatim `recentEvents` window (short range, render-capped at 20). No lossy rewrite anywhere in the loop; one fewer call-type and one fewer blocking call on the turns compaction used to fire.

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| **LIFE-MEM-1** | **No LLM memory-rewrite on the turn loop.** `life_step` is the only per-turn call; `life_compact` is deleted. Durable narrative memory is the append-only `storyLog` (`LIFE-STORY-LOG-2`) authored FREE inside `life_step`'s `logLine`, plus the append-only `canon` ledger (`LIFE-CANON-1`) and per-NPC `oneLineMemory` (`LIFE-NPC-MEM-1`, also inline). The prompt body is bounded by construction (SI sent separately; `<recentEvents>` render-capped to 20 — `LIFE-CTX-13`), so there is no runaway to compact. The single biggest risk — a marathon chapter (>60 nodes) losing non-canon, non-NPC developments from the deterministic `capStoryLog` middle-drop — is mitigated by the raised cap (60/head 8, past essentially every real chapter) and is strictly better than the old rewrite, which had that lossiness PLUS the `LIFE-COMPACT-6` through-line-drop bug. Escalation seam (not shipped): if telemetry shows loss, fold the dropped-middle `logLine`s into a single frozen `<earlierThisChapter>` digest at cap time — non-blocking, never a per-turn compaction. |
| **LIFE-MEM-2** | **RETIRED 2026-09-29 — `rollingSummary` is gone.** The batch job cleared it from every `lifeWorlds` doc; `ZLifeWorld` no longer has the field, `createLifeWorld` does not write it, the step spec / resume-recap spec carry no `rollingSummary` / `legacySummary`, and `WurldResumeScreen` falls back from `resumeRecap.text` to the `storyLog` spine only. *(Historical text:)* `rollingSummary` is a retained legacy field, read-only. `ZLifeWorld.rollingSummary` STAYS in the schema (`z.string().default('')`) so a world created before WS1-C still renders — and so `WurldResumeScreen`'s non-optional `.trim()` never throws — but NOTHING writes it anymore. It surfaces in exactly two read-only shims that retire deterministically: (a) the step prompt renders a `<priorSummary>` block ONLY when `rollingSummary` is non-empty AND `storyLog` has `< LEGACY_SUMMARY_SHIM_MAX` (8) entries — a new world (`''`) never trips it, and a legacy world drops it once its log fills; (b) the resume recap + `WurldResumeScreen` prefer the generated `resumeRecap.text`, then the `storyLog` spine, then `rollingSummary` last. |
| **LIFE-NPC-MEM-1** *(see §7)* | Per-NPC `oneLineMemory` now authored inline by `life_step` (`npcMemoryUpdates`), not by compaction. The shared `writeNpcMemories` helper is retained; only its compaction caller was deleted. |

---

## 13. System Event log — `LIFE-EVENT-*`

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| **LIFE-EVENT-1** | `lifeEvents` is append-only. Every meaningful happening writes a row: beat (narrator/NPC speech), decision (user picked X), flashcard_added, npc_introduced, backdrop_visited, correction (graded-wrong answer + the corrected form). Rows are NEVER edited; updates land as new rows. |
| **LIFE-EVENT-2** | The LLM sees events as XML/structured blocks in the recent-events section of the prompt (`LIFE-CTX-6`). The data model + the prompt rendering are decoupled — adding new event kinds is a spec + schema change, not a prompt restructure. |
| **LIFE-EVENT-3** | Each event row is < 2KB. Beat events store the bilingual payload (practice + native + complexWords + wordTranslations) so historical bubbles can be re-rendered with full translation chrome if surfaced later (e.g. a "story so far" recap screen — future feature). |
| **LIFE-EVENT-4** | The event log is queryable: `where('createdAt', '>', X).orderBy('createdAt')` is the only query the orchestrator runs against it. No composite indexes beyond the default. |
| **LIFE-EVENT-5** | Adding a new flashcard during a `/life` session writes BOTH a `flashcard_added` event (so future LLM context sees it) AND the regular `flashcards/{cardId}` doc (so /cards sees it). Both writes are non-atomic; the flashcards-side is authoritative if they diverge. |

---

## 14. Character sheet + customisation — `LIFE-SETTINGS-*`

Surfaces the user's character + world state for editing.

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| **LIFE-SETTINGS-1** | Route: `/life/me`. Shows the user's avatar, character sheet (name + age + traits + city + worldSeed), NPC roster with avatars + one-line memories, scene gallery with thumbnails. |
| **LIFE-SETTINGS-2** | Edits to character sheet fields hit `lifeWorld` directly and bust the prompt cache from the character-sheet block onward. Changes are reflected in the next `lifeStep` call. |
| **LIFE-SETTINGS-3** | Avatar regeneration: a "regenerate avatar" affordance with a free-text delta prompt ("change my hair to red"). Rate-limited per `LIFE-BACKDROP-10`. |
| **LIFE-SETTINGS-4** | NPC management: per-NPC rename, edit traits/memory, delete (writes `npc_forgotten` event, removes from roster). |
| **LIFE-SETTINGS-5** | Scene management: per-persistent-scene preview, regenerate ("rebuild this café"), forget (remove from index — the model treats it as "you've never been there before"). |
| **LIFE-SETTINGS-6** | "Restart life" affordance (with two-step confirm): wipes `lifeWorld`, `lifeEvents`, `lifeNpcs`, `lifeBackdrops`, and Storage assets. Flashcards persist. Returns to onboarding. |
| **LIFE-SETTINGS-7** | **Per-wurld difficulty override.** A wurld's difficulty DEFAULTS to following the live global `profile.difficulty` (`ZLifeWorld.difficultyPinned` absent/`null`), so a mid-story change in Settings re-resolves and applies on the next generation. From the ⋯ menu's Difficulty section the owner can PIN the wurld to a fixed 1-7 level (`updateLifeWorld(uid, worldId, { difficultyPinned: N })`) or return it to following global (`{ difficultyPinned: null }`) — both reachable whether currently automatic or pinned. The effective level is `resolveWurldDifficulty(difficultyPinned, profile.difficulty)` = `difficultyPinned ?? global ?? 2`, resolved ONCE at the LifeScreen props boundary (`useEffectiveWorld`) and threaded into every generator via the node engine's `world` prop; the legacy `difficulty` snapshot is never re-frozen (world-delta writes never touch it), and wurlds created before this override (no `difficultyPinned`) follow global. The menu renders the AUTOMATIC state ("Following global · Level X: {label}", X = current global, label = `settings.difficulty.level{X}.label`) when unpinned and the pinned level when pinned; the 1-7 picker reuses the `settings.difficulty.level{N}.label` names (it does NOT reuse `DifficultyControl` wholesale — that component is `profile.difficulty`-coupled). |

---

## 15. Local-first LLM dev — `LIFE-DEV-*`

During the vertical slice, we iterate on prompts without redeploying Cloud Functions. After the slice solidifies, the same prompt files move into `functions/`.

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| **LIFE-DEV-1** | A `lifeLLM.ts` shim exposes one interface: `generate({ prompt, schema, thinkingLevel }) => Response`. Two implementations exist behind a build-time flag (`VITE_LIFE_LLM_MODE`): `local` calls Gemini directly from the browser using a dev-only API key, `cloud` calls the `lifeStep` / `generateBackdrop` / `lifeCompact` Cloud Functions. |
| **LIFE-DEV-2** | The `local` implementation is GATED to `import.meta.env.DEV`. It throws on production builds. The dev API key is read from `.env.local` (gitignored) and is NEVER bundled in production. |
| **LIFE-DEV-3** | Prompt files live in `src/lib/life/prompts/` as plain TS exports during the slice phase. When ready to harden, these files MOVE (git-mv, not copy) to `functions/src/prompts/life/` and the cloud wrappers take over. The spec calls out the move explicitly so we don't end up with two copies drifting. |
| **LIFE-DEV-4** | Both implementations honour the same response schema. Switching modes never changes app behaviour — only latency and cost. A toggle in the dev console (debug-only menu) lets the developer flip between `local` and `cloud` at runtime, for A/B sanity checks. |

---

## 16. Vertical-slice WIP rules — `LIFE-WIP-*`

Anything unfinished must visibly look unfinished, never broken.

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| **LIFE-WIP-1** | The slice will land in phases; each phase ships a usable subset. Phase A: scene gen + cache + fallback library + a hardcoded narrator bubble + a hardcoded chip grid that loops back to itself. Phase B: real `lifeStep` integration + the two-bubble window + persistent event log. Phase C: NPC/scene transience + compaction + transport loop. Phase D: graded exercises + save-concept + character sheet. |
| **LIFE-WIP-2** | Every screen affordance that isn't yet wired up MUST render with a visible `WIP` badge AND a one-line caption (`"Avatar customisation — coming soon"`). Bug-looking states are forbidden: no empty bubbles, no broken-image placeholders, no missing-asset icons, no `[object Object]` strings. |
| **LIFE-WIP-3** | Features that are deferred entirely render NOTHING (no shadow of where they'll go) unless the absence breaks layout. Half-rendered chrome is forbidden. The choice is binary: ship the affordance with a `WIP` chip, or omit it. |
| **LIFE-WIP-4** | The WIP badge style is consistent across the screen: a small text-pill `WIP` in `--color-fg-muted` with a tooltip giving the one-line caption. NEVER red or warning-coloured (would read as error). |
| **LIFE-WIP-5** | At every slice phase boundary, the spec MUST be updated to remove the corresponding `WIP` markers and add the proper invariant bodies. No `WIP` lingers past its phase. |

---

## 17. Failure handling — `LIFE-FAIL-*`

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| **LIFE-FAIL-1** | `lifeStep` schema-violation: ONE retry with the validation errors attached as a system note. On second failure, surface a recoverable error inline (`"Something feels strange. Try again?"` with a retry button) and DO NOT advance the world. |
| **LIFE-FAIL-2** | `lifeStep` network failure: optimistic user bubble + thinking-dots stay rendered; an inline retry pill appears under the user bubble after 8s; the user can tap it to re-fire the same `lifeStep` call (same `stepId` → idempotent). |
| **LIFE-FAIL-3** | Persistent scene gen failure: ONE retry with the failure mode attached as a system note (e.g. "previous response failed XML validation"). On second failure, the scene's row in `lifeBackdrops` is marked `failed: true` AND the orchestrator pivots to the fallback library's silhouette for that location category filled with the user's avatar. The next visit retries from scratch. |
| **LIFE-FAIL-4** | Transient scene gen failure: NO retry. Skip directly to the fallback silhouette for that category. Cheap renders aren't worth the cost of retrying. |
| **LIFE-FAIL-5** | Persistent NPC avatar gen failure: ONE retry. On failure, the NPC keeps the worldSeed-themed silhouette permanently. A "regenerate avatar" affordance in `LIFE-SETTINGS-*` lets the user re-attempt. |
| **LIFE-FAIL-6** | Transient NPC avatar gen: never attempted — they always show the silhouette (`LIFE-NPC-3`). |
| **LIFE-FAIL-7** | Compaction failure: the un-compacted context is used for the next call. Compaction is retried on the call AFTER the next one. If three consecutive compaction attempts fail, the orchestrator surfaces a soft warning in the character sheet ("the world's memory is getting noisy — try a regenerate?") but does NOT block play. |
| **LIFE-FAIL-8** | All failures log to Sentry through the shared `lib/log.ts` (`OBS-*`), with `feature: 'life-sim'` tags so the admin dashboard can spot regressions. |
| **LIFE-FAIL-9** | **No avatar shimmers forever — every `pending` avatar reaches a terminal state.** Two guards close the silent-failure paths that previously left the player/NPC avatar slot shimmering permanently when a gen died mid-flight (a reload/navigation after the status write hit `'pending'` but before it settled). (a) **Player-avatar pending-recovery** (`commitNode`): the player-avatar kickoff now fires on a `null` status (never attempted) OR a stale `'pending'` — the old `characterAvatarStatus == null` gate never re-fired, so a dead `'pending'` was permanent. A module-level `inFlightPlayerAvatar` guard (keyed by worldId, added synchronously before the first `await`) dedups a genuinely in-flight gen this session, so a live-but-slow gen is never double-fired; `'ready'`/`'failed'` are terminal and skip. The gen is re-attempted on the next node arrival (the module guard is empty after a reload), mirroring the `LIFE-NPC-6` roster sweep that already re-kicks stale-`'pending'` NPCs. (b) **Client-side pending timeout** (`useCharacterAvatar` / `useNpcAvatarPending`, `PENDING_AVATAR_TIMEOUT_MS = 45s`): a `'pending'` status that outlives the timeout is treated as given-up — the shimmer stops and the **initials circle** shows (the graceful terminal fallback of `LIFE-NPC-2`), rather than shimmering forever, even if the player takes no further action. 45s is far beyond any live gen (parts ~2-5s, direct draw ~8×), so a fired timeout means the call died; if a slow gen DOES land later the status flips to `'ready'` and the real avatar overrides the initials. This REFINES `LIFE-NPC-2`'s "`npcAvatarPending`/`userAvatarLoading` is driven by `avatarStatus === 'pending'`" to "…`=== 'pending'` AND still inside the client timeout window". **Retries:** the previously-unimplemented `LIFE-FAIL-3` (persistent scene: ONE retry before fallback) and `LIFE-FAIL-5` (persistent NPC avatar: ONE retry before `'failed'`) are now wired — `generateBackdrop` retries once for a persistent descriptor (transient skips, `LIFE-FAIL-4`), and `generateNpcAvatar` is wrapped in one retry (`generateNpcAvatarWithRetry`) for both the player and NPC kickoffs before the terminal `'failed'` write. |

## 19. Future / out of scope

Carved out of v1; revisit if user feedback indicates demand:

- **Multiple lives per user.** v1 ships one life per user, "restart" is the only way to start over. Multi-life requires a list UI + life selector — easy follow-up.
- **Voice input.** TTS is in (we reuse the existing singleton). Speech-to-text answers are future.
- **Multiplayer.** Two users sharing a world is a different product.
- **Long-term arc generation.** v1 is moment-to-moment beats. Multi-day quest arcs ("you have a job interview Friday") need a sim-time scheduling layer.
- **Per-user art style customisation.** v1 lets Gemini pick the style. A "I want my world in watercolour" knob is future.
- **Music / sound design.** Atmospheric audio is a heavy lift; deferred.
- **Hand-drawn character avatar overlay in scenes.** v1 is first-person only — the user never sees themselves in the scene. Optional first-person hand props are a v1.1+ delight.
- **Inline mini-SVG vocab props in the speech bubble.** When introducing "mangue", a tiny mango icon next to the word. Nice; complex.
- **Sentence-construction, fill-blank, listening exercise types.** v1 is multi-choice only (`LIFE-EX-5`). Adding more requires new ActionBar states.
- **Explicit Gemini `cachedContent` cache for `lifeStep`.** Plan: at chapter start, package the stable bundle (system instruction + saga + chapter outline + initial roster + frozen flashcard deck, practice side only, unarchived) into a `cachedContent` blob. Save the cache id on the world doc. Each `lifeStep` call references the id + sends only the volatile tail (recent events + current input). The flashcard set is frozen for the chapter; new cards added mid-chapter wait for the next chapter to enter the cache. Cache lifecycle: created on chapter-start, deleted on (a) chapter end, (b) **world exit** (user navigates away from `/life/{worldId}`), and (c) cache TTL expiry. Telemetry tab in the debug overlay (already live) gives empirical "is the implicit cache hitting" data — required before we commit to building this. Spec: schema-ready via `consecutiveCorrectDays` + `cardStatus` already in `ZFlashcard`; needs a `flashcardId` field on `practicePrompt` so the model can echo which card it used.

### Wurlds v2 backlog (formalized 2026-07)

Deferred from the Wurlds v2 plan (deleted 2026-09-30, audit Q-9; read it with `git show 9c70919:docs/archive/wurlds-v2/00-approved-plan.md`). Each item carries its trigger for revisiting; none is scheduled. This section is the canonical home — the plan file is not.

- **Wurld-mistakes practice session.** A practice-mode session seeded from questions the player got WRONG in wurlds — `correction` events with `correct: false` carry the tested answer (`correctAnswer`) + the prompt context (`promptSource`), ; feed them into `buildSession` as a "Review your wurld mistakes" intent (practice mode's session builder + card selector already support intents). The full loop: story exposes → wurld tests → practice mode drills the misses. *Revisit when:* a user asks how to review their wurld mistakes, or telemetry shows sustained sub-70% graded accuracy in wurlds.
- **Listening question node** (listen-and-pick lift). TTS plays the NPC line with the text hidden; native options. A new node kind that slots into the `LIFE-NODE-*` taxonomy cleanly; uses the existing `TTSButton` / `synthesizeSpeech`. *Revisit when:* the Phase F node engine has settled in production (no schema churn for a few weeks) and per-session TTS cost is known.
- **Recap quiz.** The resume-recap screen ends with one comprehension question about last session ("What did Mathieu ask you to do?") — memory + language reinforcement in one. Needs a small gen call at recap time. *Revisit when:* the Phase C resume screen has shipped and resume telemetry shows players actually read it.
- **Wurld sessions feed practice mode.** Pipe wurld `correction`-event topics into practice mode's `recentSessionTopics` recency signal (the "Coordinates with" note at the top of this spec anticipates this; not yet wired). *Revisit when:* active wurld players report repetitive practice sessions, or the next change touches `PRACTICE-PLAN-*` recency inputs anyway.
- **Chapter transcript view.** Re-read a chapter's full dialogue (the events log already has everything, zero LLM) with vocab taps live — re-reading comprehensible input is one of the cheapest high-value learning modes. *Revisit when:* a user asks to re-read a chapter, or we want a "Re-read" CTA beside the chapter-complete stats.
- **Difficulty auto-tune per wurld.** Client computes rolling graded-question accuracy; the prompt carries a one-line nudge ("player is at 92% — stretch them" / "at 45% — simplify structures"). One SI line + one derived stat. *Revisit when:* playtests show worlds pinned too easy or too hard for more than a session.
- **Per-wurld fluency stat.** RPG progression derived from correction-event accuracy — "your French Charisma is growing". *Revisit when:* difficulty auto-tune lands (same derived-accuracy input) and the wurld card needs a visible progression hook.
- **80-locale i18n regen.** User-triggered `npm run i18n:regen`; costs cents but touches 80 files. *Revisit when:* the wurlds-v2 `en.ts` strings have stabilised (after Phase H lands).
- **Explicit Gemini `cachedContent` caching.** Already formalized above in this section (the `cachedContent` bullet) — not duplicated here. *Revisit when:* the debug Telemetry tab shows the implicit prefix cache degrading (hit-rate drop / cost-per-turn regression).

---

## 20. Design decisions

### Why backdrop-first, not chat-first

The original proposal framed the experience as a chat thread. Early design iteration concluded the experience needs to **feel like an RPG**, not a messaging app. The scene IS the headline feature — without it, this is just a slightly fancier tutor. Scene-first composition (full-bleed SVG, overlaid bubble, action bar at the bottom) is structurally different enough that we don't reuse `ChatScreen` / `MessageList` chrome. We DO reuse the bubble-level translation + vocab + ExplainModal infrastructure (`MSG-VIEW-*`, `MSG-VOCAB-*`) because that's per-bubble and identical across surfaces.

### Why per-world backdrop cache (not global)

A global cache (every user's "fruit market in France" is the same SVG) would maximise reuse and minimise cost. But narratively important: "your favourite café" should always look like YOUR favourite café across sessions, not a generic one. The cost of per-world caching is acceptable because (a) returning visits are still free, (b) within one world the user only visits ~20-50 unique persistent locations before they're satisfied, (c) Gemini SVG gen is cheap-ish even at `thinkingLevel: 'medium'`. We can introduce a global fallback cache for truly generic scenes (a generic train station, a generic airport) later if costs become an issue.

### Why transient/persistent at the step generator, not at compaction

The step generator already understands the narrative arc — whether the fruit seller is "a recurring character whose café you'll return to" or "a one-line throwaway who tells you the time" is information it has at generation time. Asking compaction to figure it out retroactively works but wastes a render for transient characters and delays avatars for persistent ones. We let the step generator flag transience as a primary path, and use compaction's retroactive promotion as the SAFETY NET when the generator misjudges (`LIFE-NPC-4`). Two layers, robust.

### Why the STAGE holds the old backdrop, rather than the hook not clearing it (LIFE-BACKDROP-15)

The blank-out has one cause: `useLifeBackdrop` sets `svg` to the bundled silhouette the instant a render starts, so the player watches their scene vanish for the 3–8s a Gemini SVG takes. The obvious fix is in the hook — keep the previous `svg` in state and only overwrite it on `'ready'`. It was NOT taken, for two reasons. (1) The hook's `svg` is consumed as "the resolved value for THIS descriptor"; making it lag the descriptor would quietly break every other reader's assumption (and `backdropBuildingVisible` reads `status`, which would then disagree with `svg`). (2) The presentational question — *what is painted while we wait* — is the stage's job, and the stage is the only place that knows what is currently on screen. Putting the memory in `BackdropStage` also means the hold survives any future producer: a transport-loop scene (`LIFE-TRANS-*`), a manual regenerate (`LIFE-BACKDROP-10`), a cache re-seed. The cost is that the stage has to CLASSIFY the incoming string rather than being told, which is why `isBundledSilhouette` is an exact match against the shipped library rather than a heuristic — a fuzzy test ("looks like a fallback") would eventually hold a real scene off-screen forever, the one failure mode worse than blanking.

Alternative considered and rejected: a `status` prop threaded from `LifeScreen`. It is more explicit, but it makes the fix undeliverable without editing `LifeScreen` (owned by other work in flight), and it does not actually carry more information than the classification does — `'generating'` and `'fallback'` both arrive carrying the same silhouette bytes, and both want the same answer ("hold"). Revisit if the stage ever needs to distinguish them, e.g. to show a retry affordance on terminal failure.

**Revisit trigger for the shimmer:** the veil is still rendered by `LifeScreen` as an `absolute inset-0` sibling of the whole scene+dialogue column, so it sweeps the dialogue too. That predates this change and was left alone. If it is ever moved, it belongs INSIDE `BackdropStage` (scoped to the scene box) — but the stage must then own it exclusively, or the two shimmers stack.

### Why a planet, and why it eats the first-gen failure case (LIFE-BACKDROP-16)

The pre-first-scene placeholder used to be the `apartment_bedroom` silhouette plus a ribbon reading "Your apartment · morning". Both are *guesses seeded from the world chip* — the world has no scene, no location, and no time of day yet, and the first beat's `backdropIntent` routinely contradicts both. Stating them as fact is worse than saying nothing: a player whose world opens in a frontier colony read "Your apartment · morning" as a bug. A planet says the only true thing available — *a world is being formed* — and says it without committing to a genre, which is why it is deliberately vague (no continents, no landmarks, no architecture). It also composes with the existing "Building your wurld…" pill instead of competing with it.

The carve-out worth flagging: the planet also stands in for the `LIFE-BACKDROP-8` category silhouette when the FIRST gen of a world fails terminally, because the stage's test is "has a real scene ever been painted here", and it cannot be anything else without a signal `LifeScreen` does not currently pass. That window is narrow (first scene of a fresh world, both gen attempts failed) and the planet is arguably the more honest picture there too — the alternative is a polished apartment that is not the player's apartment. But it IS a narrowing of `LIFE-BACKDROP-8`, recorded in that invariant. Revisit if a retry affordance is ever added to the failure path, since that surface would need to distinguish the two states anyway.

### Why CSS-class time-of-day instead of separate renders

Generating a "morning fruit market", "midday fruit market", "evening fruit market", "night fruit market" as four separate SVGs is 4x the render cost AND breaks per-world cache reuse (now we have four cache entries per location). Tagging elements at gen time and applying CSS variants is structurally cheaper. The reliability risk (does Gemini consistently apply the classes?) is mitigated by the overlay fallback (`LIFE-BACKDROP-6`). We start with tags; if the failure rate is too high in practice, the spec calls out the overlay path as a documented fallback.

### Why local-first LLM dev during the slice

Cloud Functions deploys are slow and expensive in iteration time. Most of the early work is prompt-tweaking, not infrastructure. A `local` mode that calls Gemini direct from the browser (dev-only, gated to `import.meta.env.DEV`) lets us iterate prompts at HMR speed. The MOVE (git-mv, not copy) of prompt files to `functions/` at the end of the slice phase ensures we don't fork into client + server copies — there's exactly one canonical prompt at any given time.

### Why two-bubble sliding window, not full chat history

A chat-style stack would compete with the scene visually and obscure the RPG identity. The two-bubble window keeps the dialogue ALWAYS readable (previous turn + current turn both visible) without growing the screen real estate over time. The user can still tap to inspect translations on both visible bubbles — that's the most-recent context they need. Historical context lives in the event log, surfaced through the character sheet's "story so far" affordance (future).

### Why no chat-style "user message" persistence after the next turn

When a NEW NPC bubble arrives, the user's prior response slides up into the top slot. When the user picks AGAIN, that response slides off-screen. The user's choices are recorded in the event log but never re-rendered in the bubble window. This preserves the two-bubble invariant AND emphasises that the experience is forward-moving — your past choices SHAPE the world (via the LLM context) but don't clutter the surface.

### Why one life per user in v1

Multi-life adds: a life selector UI, per-life Storage namespacing, per-life event/NPC/scene collections, switching state. Each adds development cost without changing the core experience. v1 ships single-life because "restart" is a complete-enough escape hatch. Multi-life lands when user feedback says "I want to try a different city without losing this one."

### Why frozen practice language

Switching practice language mid-life would invalidate the entire event log (the LLM cannot read events in language A when the world's language is now B). Cheapest correct behaviour: lock the world's language at creation. Users who want a different language start a new life (or wait for multi-life, see above). This also lets the LLM context safely assume "every event row is in this one language pair" without per-event language fields.

### Why bubble vocab taps skip chips and the practice-prompt card (Phase E)

Chips already carry a primary tap action — pick the option. Per-word taps inside a chip would create two competing gestures on a small target, so chips render plain; once a chip's line lands as a bubble at practice resolution, its words become tappable there (same information, one gesture per surface). The `PracticePromptCard` is excluded entirely: on a graded turn the card IS the question, and a tap-to-gloss on its words would hand the player the answer — the same reasoning as `hideNativeForUtterance`, which therefore also suppresses vocab taps on the comprehension-source bubble until the player spends the reveal. Annotations bind to the practice line only: in `native-only` viewMode the bubble's primary line is the native text, which renders plain — underlining native words to show practice-side glosses would invert the learning direction for no benefit. The save path stamps `"<practiceText>" — <speaker>, <location>` into the card's `context` so a review weeks later recalls the scene the word came from. Unsave resolves the card id from the live deck lookup rather than a per-session ref (chat's approach) so the toggle still works after remount/resume; the trade-off is that ANY deck card with the same phrase (case-insensitive, same practice language) reads as "saved" — acceptable, since deleting that card is what an unsave should do anyway. Revisit if per-word saves need to be scoped to their source bubble.

### Why the resume recap assembles from stored data (wurlds-v2 Phase C)

The recap screen must be INSTANT — it renders in the same frame as the world it overlays, offline-tolerant, and free. Every field it shows is already persisted (saga teaser, arc premise, rollingSummary, beat events), so the resume path makes zero LLM calls; the only generation is a single flash-lite polish call fired-and-forgotten on world EXIT, where its latency is invisible and its failure costs nothing (the screen falls back to rollingSummary, then hides the section). Exit-not-entry also means the recap describes the session that just ended — exactly the "Previously on…" content. The ≥5-new-events gate (anchored on `resumeRecap.lastEventId`) keeps a quick peek-and-leave from burning a call; an out-of-window anchor id counts as stale because at least a full listener window of events has landed since. Session-open recording moves with the Continue tap for the same reason: a recap peek-and-leave counts as neither a generation trigger nor a session day — `recordSessionOpen` (streak tracking) fires on recap-Continue, mirroring the fresh-world flow where it already sits behind the intro's Begin. `resumeRecap.text` stores a SINGLE system-language paragraph (the "Previously" recap is narration register — `LIFE-LANG-8` — so it reads in the world's system language only; a bilingual recap doubled the reading load for no benefit). One `text` field keeps the world-doc schema flat and the fallback chain (`text → rollingSummary → hide`) one-dimensional. The generator SI is parameterised on the effective system language (`world.systemLanguage ?? world.nativeLanguage`); the "Moments ago" mini-bubbles carry the per-line practice + system-language gloss separately (`LIFE-FLOW-5`). The call uses DEFAULT temperature (not `LIFE_TEMPERATURE` 0.95): recap is faithful summarisation, the same stance compaction takes. Revisit trigger: the "recap quiz" backlog item wants a comprehension question at recap time — that adds a gen call on the resume path and should re-open this decision.

### Why the two-channel language model (narration=system / dialogue=practice)

Leveling narration to difficulty made every beginner wurld read like a graded reader — flat, atmosphere-free prose, because "short high-frequency sentences" is death for scene-setting. But leveling is exactly right for the DIALOGUE the player is meant to practise. Splitting the two channels lets the model write beautiful, complex system-language narration (comprehension isn't the goal there — the player reads it in a language they already know) while keeping the spoken practice-language lines at the player's level. The `systemLanguage` is captured from the active UI locale (not `nativeLanguage`) so a user who runs the app in a third language gets narration they can actually read; `resolveSystemLanguage` PREFERS the user's own `nativeLanguage`/`practiceLanguage` name strings when the locale maps to them so the downstream `=== practiceLanguage` immersion gate and `=== nativeLanguage` warning gate are exact string matches, not fuzzy. `nativeText` was NOT renamed to `systemText` despite now meaning "system-language line" — it threads through `ZStepBeat` → beat events → `resumeBubblesFromEvents` → the render, and a rename would churn every one of those plus the persisted event payloads for zero behaviour change. Revisit trigger: if a `systemText` alias becomes worth the migration once the two-channel model settles.

### Why the vocab recap derives from the loaded event window (not a fresh query)

The "Words you practised" list (LIFE-VOCAB-RECAP-*) filters the `useLifeEvents` window (last 40 events) by the scene/chapter start timestamp instead of running a `where('createdAt','>', windowStart)` query for the full range. Alternatives weighed: a one-shot range query would make the chapter list complete for long chapters (100–300 event reads per chapter end), but the completion STATS already run on the same 40-event window (`snapshotSceneCompletion` receives the listener's `eventsData`), so a fuller word list would sit next to knowingly-bounded stats — inconsistent, and the celebration overlay is a recap, not an audit. Zero extra reads wins for v1. Trigger for revisiting: if chapter recaps regularly look truncated in playtests (players practise far more than ~40 events per chapter and notice words missing), switch BOTH the stats and the words to a shared range query in one change.

### Open question — native thinking on the life-sim call sites (2026-07-25)

**Status: RESOLVED for `generateBackdrop`, `runWorldSagaGenerate` and `generateStepOptions` (life_step) (2026-07-25). Five sites remain.**

| site | was | now | evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| `generateBackdrop` | `medium` | **`minimal`** | thinking = 59% of the call; drawing FELL from 43 to 25 nodes at `medium`. Worse on both axes. |
| `runWorldSagaGenerate` | `high` | **`minimal`** | thinking = 920 tok (32% of the call), 2x latency (17.1s -> 8.2s), and NO measurable gain: JSON 3/3 either way, same 17 keys, same 0/3 cliche titles. Reading the prose, `high` reached for a named villain (the shape LIFE-STORY-SHAPE-7 forbids) where `minimal` produced systemic opposing forces. |
| `runSceneGenerate`, `runWorldArcGenerate` | `high` | unchanged | not yet A/B'd |
| `generateStepOptions` (life_step) | `medium` | **`minimal`** | N=4/level on the real 13.5k-token prompt with cache-aware pricing: schema validity 4/4 at EVERY level, so nothing was bought — while `medium` cost 16.5% more and **2.9x the latency (8557ms -> 2912ms) on the one call the player waits on every turn**. `low` was worse than `medium` (925 vs 610 thinking tokens) — emission is non-deterministic, so the only stable setting is off. Thinking also counts against `maxOutputTokens`, so it can truncate the JSON and burn a retry (observed once at `medium`). |
| `runSceneReplan`, `generateBio` | `medium` | unchanged | not yet A/B'd |
| `runGradedQuestionGenerate`, `runResumeRecapGenerate` | `low` | unchanged | not yet A/B'd |

THREE independent sites now show native thinking costing 16-59% of the call for no measurable gain, across SVG, JSON prose and JSON game-state — so the result is not an artefact of one output format. The per-turn site adds a latency argument on top of cost. Treat the remaining six as guilty until measured, but measure them: the graded-question site in particular has a correctness contract that reasoning might genuinely serve.

**Saga model, still open:** `LIFE_SAGA_MODEL` is `gemini-3-flash-preview` ($0.50/$3.00). At `minimal` it costs $0.005658/call; `gemini-3.5-flash-lite` costs $0.004323 for the same structural quality (-24%), but its titles read blander in a 3-seed sample ("La Peinture d'Hiver" vs "Le Dernier Pigment de l'Hiver"). Judgement call, not measured to a conclusion. `gemini-3.1-flash-lite` is NOT safe here — 2/3 valid JSON and only 11 of 17 keys.

The life-sim client sets a native `thinkingLevel` at nine call sites —
`runWorldSagaGenerate` / `runWorldArcGenerate` / `runSceneGenerate`
(`high`), `runSceneReplan` / `generateBio` /
`generateStepOptions` (`medium`; `generateBackdrop` was here and is now `minimal`), `runGradedQuestionGenerate` /
`runResumeRecapGenerate` (`low`). The value is forwarded end-to-end:
client body -> `functions/src/index.ts` `geminiGenerate` -> `getGeminiModel`
-> `generationConfig.thinkingConfig.thinkingLevel`.

This sits in tension with the design intent expressed by
`NO_INTERNAL_THINKING_DIRECTIVE` (`functions/src/prompts/unifiedAnalysis.ts`),
which the message/tutor prompts use to force reasoning into visible XML
so it can be GUIDED, and with the stated intent that all life-sim
reasoning likewise lives in structured output. Because `includeThoughts`
defaults to `false` on the client path, any native thinking that IS
produced is generated, billed, and then discarded unread — measured at
1064 thinking tokens on one `high` call (~$0.0016, ~52% of that call's
true cost, and invisible before OBS-USAGE-2).

**Scene result, as a prior for the rest:** turning it off was strictly better — 59% of that call's cost was thinking, and removing it INCREASED drawing. That does not automatically generalise (scene output is SVG, the story calls are prose), but it shifts the burden of proof. **Why the rest are not simply turned off:** the levels may be doing real work
on the one-shot creative calls (saga, scene, arc), and no A/B has been
run on life-sim OUTPUT QUALITY with them at `minimal`. Turning them off
is a cost win only if quality holds — untested.

**Next step (pick one):**
1. A/B each site at its current level vs `minimal`, scored on the same
   real-Gemini basis used for LIFE-BACKDROP-11, then set `minimal` wherever
   quality holds; or
2. If the levels ARE load-bearing, update the design intent to say so
   explicitly — native thinking is used for one-shot creative generation,
   guided XML thinking for per-turn structured output — and document why
   the two coexist.

Until then the levels stay as-is and OBS-USAGE-2 ensures their cost is at
least VISIBLE rather than silently under-reported.

---

## Engine comparison — `LIFE-ENGINE-CMP-*` (STUB: findings only, no behaviour)

Written 2026-08-09 after reading the parallel story-engine-v2 branch
(`origin/claude/life-sim-wurlds-review-hs6y2m`, `docs/specs/story-engine-v2.md`,
901 lines, revisions 0 → F).

**v2 IS INSPIRATION, NOT A WORKING MODEL.** An earlier draft of this section
adopted its deadline design as established and had to be deleted before it
landed. That draft cited v2's own numbers as proof, which is exactly the mistake
`LIFE-CONTRACT-EX-9` had just recorded — treating a plausible mechanism as
demonstrated. The correction below is what the branch actually says when read to
the end rather than to the part that agreed with the plan.

### `LIFE-ENGINE-CMP-1` — the instrument is the finding

**The single most valuable thing on that branch is not a design, it is a
measurement about measurement**, and it invalidates most of the rest.

> §B.6: *"the instrument was not trustworthy, and nothing before this is safe to
> believe."*

Two consecutive 24-turn runs of **identical code** on the same fixture scored
**3.38 and 2.38**, and the gate flipped between them. The same saved transcript
judged four times spreads **0.00–0.25** — so the JUDGE is a good ruler and the
RUNS are what differ. Twenty-four turns of sampling compound into genuinely
better and worse stories; the engine emits a *distribution* and a single run is a
coin flip.

**Consequences for us, and they are the reason this row exists:**

- **Every single-run judge number in that document — and in ours — is
  uninterpretable at a resolution finer than ~±0.5.** That includes the series
  `2.43 → 2.29 → 2.14` which the deleted draft cited as proof that *variety*
  fails. The whole series fits inside the noise band. It is not evidence.
- **`BENCH-1` (median-of-N judging) is now a PREREQUISITE, not a follow-up.** No
  engine change — ours or theirs — can be accepted or rejected until the ruler
  is trustworthy. v2's own answer is to read the MEDIAN of N runs for judged
  metrics and the WORST run for contract metrics (a parse failure in one run of
  three is a parse failure).

### `LIFE-ENGINE-CMP-2` — what v2 TESTED, and what it only PROPOSED

Read to the end, the branch is a record of three hypotheses about agency, two of
which were measured and neither of which passed:

| revision | the idea | measured result |
|---|---|---|
| **0** | `ENGAGE / DEEPEN / LEAVE` — three subjects, all free | **AGENCY 2/5** |
| **A** | the deadline + `ADVANCE / INVEST / SIDESTEP` trilemma; options *cost* | **AGENCY still 2/5 on every run**, including runs that passed the FUN gate |
| **B** | exposure — an `ADVANCE` that *can fail* | median-of-3: AGENCY **3** (lyon) / **2** (gallery). Gate was ≥3 on BOTH — **did not pass** |

So **scarcity was tried and did not fix agency**, which is precisely the claim
the deleted draft was about to write into this file as settled. Revision B's
diagnosis of why is the part worth keeping, because it is an argument rather than
a score: *every option succeeded* — `driveWorld` completed an `ADVANCE`'s step
unconditionally and `reachOf` was computed, exported, and never called — so the
grid was three ways to win differing only in price, and **a trade-off you always
win is not a decision.** That reasoning stands on its own; the number attached to
it does not yet.

Revisions run to **F**, and F.4 leaves open a defect worth knowing about before
we copy the architecture: the beat and the option grid are written in one call
and nothing constrains them to agree, so the model wrote an NPC asking *"where
are the 1954 maps?"* and simultaneously labelled a chip *"Ask Arthur for the 1954
maps"*. Their own note: *"Do not ship a fix for this on the strength of one
good-looking transcript."*

### `LIFE-ENGINE-CMP-3` — what IS safe to carry over

Only things measured HERE, or structural observations that need no judge:

- **Cost is not a differentiator.** `LIFE-CTX-17`/`-18`, our own measurement:
  v1 is ~76% implicitly cached on a fast turn and 0% past a two-minute gap —
  $0.00137/turn warm, $0.00284 cold, against v2's reported $0.0018–0.0020. v2's
  prompt module reaches the same conclusion independently (*"cut for QUALITY …
  and NOT for cost"*). **Closed as a reason to change engines.**
- **Prompt size is a control problem, and this needs no judge to see.** v1's
  `life_step` SI is **75,203 chars / 641 lines / 22+ sections**; v2's is
  **~9,569 chars / 7 sections**. The evidence that ours is too big to steer is
  `LIFE-CONTRACT-EX-9`: one rule stated twice, contradicted, exemplified
  backwards and rewritten three times across three real runs, with behaviour
  flat at 18.9 / 19.1 / 18.2%. Retry rate is a CONTRACT metric — a count, not a
  judgement — so unlike the story scores it is readable from a single run.
- **38.1% of v1's SI (28,627 chars over 80 lines) is option/grid rules.** A
  measurement of our own file. If code ever owns the grid, that is the size of
  the deletion — but note it is a size fact, NOT evidence that code-owned grids
  produce better stories, which is the thing v2 tried twice without clearing.
- **Two layers v1 lacks entirely** — an **AGENDA** (what the narrator is *for*)
  and a **PRECEDENCE** line (*what the player just did > the outcome you were
  given > what the scene is about > everything else*). v1 resolves rule
  conflicts one at a time, forever; nothing in 641 lines says which rule wins.
  Cheap, structural, and testable against the retry-rate contract metric rather
  than the judge.

### `LIFE-BENCH-7` — our own run-to-run variance, measured

`LIFE-ENGINE-CMP-1` said no story change could be believed until the ruler was
checked. It has now been checked **on our benchmark**, and the answer is not the
one v2's numbers implied — it is better in one place and worse in another, and
the difference decides how every future A/B is read.

**Method.** Four independent runs of UNCHANGED code (HEAD), one fixture
(`heist`, 3 scenes × 8 turns), judged normally. Offline aggregation by
`tests/playthrough/runVariance.ts`. This is the RUN-to-run instrument; the
existing `judgeVariance.probe.ts` measures JUDGE-to-judge on a fixed transcript
and already reported **0.00 on every dimension** — the judge does not wobble,
so everything below is the story genuinely differing.

**The composite is stable. Individual dimensions are not.**

| | spread across 4 identical runs |
|---|---|
| **COMPOSITE** | **0.08** — `[2.35, 2.38, 2.42, 2.41]`, median 2.39 |
| `Q-CADENCE`, `Q-DIRECTION`, `OPT-SPREAD`, `INTENT-MOVED`, `CAST-DEPTH`, `BACKDROP-FIT`, `BIO-FIT` | **1.00** |
| `Q-TIMING`, `OPT-LEAK`, `INTRIGUE` | 0.67 |
| `INTENT`, `LEARN-VALUE`, `CHARACTER` | 0.33 |
| `PROSE`, `COHERENCE`, `CLICHE`, `HUMOUR`, `DRAMA-FIT`, `Q-QUALITY`, `OPT-FREEDOM`, `QUESTION-VALUE`, `CLICHE-NAME` | 0.00 |

**Why the composite survives what its parts do not:** it is a mean over ~22
dimensions whose noise is largely independent, so the per-dimension swings
cancel. That is a real property, not luck — but it is also why a composite can
sit still while the thing you changed moved something underneath it.

**The operating rules this produces:**

- **A composite delta above ~0.15 is readable from a SINGLE run per arm.**
  Retroactively, this rescues the composite comparisons already drawn (e.g.
  2.55/2.48/2.48 against a 2.176 baseline) — a 0.3 delta against 0.08.
- **No single-run PER-DIMENSION comparison is safe on the seven 1.00-spread
  dimensions, and marginal at 0.67/0.33.** Conclusions of the shape "INTENT
  1.63 → 1.13 is a regression" were drawn against a 0.33 spread and were
  unearned. `BENCH-1`'s median-of-N is required before any per-dimension gate.
- **Nine dimensions have 0.00 spread and can be compared directly** —
  `PROSE`, `COHERENCE`, `CLICHE`, `HUMOUR`, `DRAMA-FIT`, `Q-QUALITY`,
  `OPT-FREEDOM`, `QUESTION-VALUE`, `CLICHE-NAME`. Prefer these when a cheap
  single-run answer is wanted.
- **CONTRACT metrics stay the cheapest currency of all** — retry rate, schema
  failures, SI size are counts, not opinions, and need no median.

**What this does NOT establish, stated because four samples is a thin base:**
one fixture, one length (24 turns), n=4. A spread of 0.00 over four runs is
evidence of *low* variance, not of zero. Longer runs and other seeds may spread
wider — `heist` may simply be a well-behaved fixture. **Re-measure before
leaning on a dimension this table calls quiet.**

**Where v2 and we differ, and it matters:** their 3.38 vs 2.38 was a
per-dimension gate on 24-turn runs — exactly the unstable kind this table
predicts. Our composite over the same length is 0.08. Both results agree on the
mechanism; only the metric being gated differs. **v2's conclusion was right for
the number they gated on and would have been wrong applied to ours.**

### `LIFE-LANG-9.1` — render the band the player is ON, not the whole ladder

**The first actual cut**, and the first instance of a mechanism worth naming:
where the SI enumerates every case of something CODE ALREADY KNOWS, only the
applicable case belongs in the prompt.

`levelLadder` shipped all three difficulty bands on every call — at level 3 the
model was handed the 1-2 rules (with a worked illustration), the 3-4 rules and
the 5-7 rules, plus a paragraph explaining the mapping. `stepSpeech`'s
`gradeTarget` rule did the same in one 692-character line. `difficulty` is a
parameter, so the choice is decidable in code, and text describing a band the
player can never reach cannot be helping — it can only compete for attention
with the band that applies. **No judged gate is needed to justify removing text
that is unreachable by construction**, which is what makes this cheaper than the
option-grid cut (38% of the SI, but blocked on code owning the grid).

**Measured: `life_step` SI 74,588 → 73,039 at level 3, −1,549 chars (2.1%);
−994 (1.3%) at level 1, where the beginner band is longest.** Modest, and
`life_step` is 88% of calls.

**A second site was tried and REVERTED, because it made the prompt BIGGER.**
The same banding applied to `gradedQuestion`'s two ladders grew that SI by
+231 chars at level 2 and +16 to +71 elsewhere: the original lines were already
dense, and giving each band its own sentence costs more preamble than the other
bands cost as clauses. Trimming the wording narrowed the gain but never closed
it. **Banding is not automatically a cut** — it is a cut only where the
enumerated cases are long relative to their framing. This is the same
non-monotonicity `LIFE-CTX-14(c)` records for cache boundaries, in a different
guise, and it was caught only by measuring at MATCHED difficulty: a first
comparison read the archive as level 3 when it was level 2 and reported a
saving that did not exist.

**Where else this mechanism applies, measured and mostly negative:** line-level
duplication is nearly absent (7 near-duplicate pairs in the whole SI, most of
them a prose rule beside its schema comment, which is legitimate); turn-kind
conditioning covers only 2.5% of the file. The remaining bulk is the OUTPUT
SCHEMA block (10.1%, of which 43% is inline comments) and the option rules
(38%). **The 8× gap to v2's SI is not recoverable by trimming — it is
structural, and it needs decisions moved into code.**

### `LIFE-SI-LEAN-1` — a 74%-smaller SI, measured

**The result the whole prompt-length argument was resting on, and it is
positive — with one correction to the instrument that has to be read first.**

`stepLean.ts` rewrites only the PROSE (73,039 → 18,865 chars, 637 lines → 147),
reusing the OUTPUT SCHEMA block, vocab rules and level ladder byte for byte so a
regression could not be blamed on a changed contract. Four runs per arm, one
fixture, split verified on the wire by SI length before anything was compared.

| | FULL (73,039) | LEAN (18,865) |
|---|---|---|
| composite, median of 4 | **2.43** `[2.30–2.47]` | **2.38** `[2.20–2.45]` |
| unparseable responses | **0 / 118** | **0 / 108** |
| mean input tokens/call | 22,190 | **8,882** (−60%) |
| mean output tokens/call | 680 | **692** (unchanged) |
| retry rate, pooled | 18.6% `[12.6, 26.6]` | 11.1% `[6.5, 18.4]` |

**What is established.** Cutting three quarters of the prose did NOT break the
parse contract — zero unparseable responses across 108 calls — and did NOT
shorten the story: output length is flat, so the model still writes as much with
a quarter of the instruction. Input drops 60% per call.

**What is NOT established.** The composite difference (0.05) and the retry
difference (intervals overlap) are both inside noise. **The lean SI is shown to
be no worse. It is not shown to be better.**

**The retry COMPOSITION changed, and that is the interesting part.**
`question-omits-lead-in` fell **20 → 3** — the defect four prompt interventions
failed to move (`LIFE-CONTRACT-EX-9`, `LIFE-PRECEDENCE-1`), apparently because
the lean SI states the lead-in rule inside the node definition instead of 130
lines away. But it did not come free: the rejections that replaced it are
`question` nodes WITH a beat (most likely `lead-in-leaks-tested-line`) and — a
real contract break — a **`continuation` node carrying TWO options when the rule
is exactly one with id `continue`.** The lean SI states that rule; the model
broke it anyway. So the lean variant trades one failure mode for another rather
than eliminating failure, and **it is not ready to ship as-is.**

**Instrument correction, and it weakens an earlier claim of mine.**
`LIFE-BENCH-7` reported composite spread of **0.08** from one set of four
identical-code runs and concluded a delta above ~0.15 was readable from a single
run. Two further 4-run sets landed at **0.17** and **0.26**. Three independent
estimates of the same quantity: 0.08 / 0.17 / 0.26. **The 0.08 was the lucky
draw, the real spread is ~0.2, and the "single run is enough above 0.15" rule
was over-claimed from n=4.** Consequences: composite comparisons need 4+ runs
per arm like everything else, and the earlier retroactive rescue of single-run
composite deltas (0.3 against 0.08) is much weaker than stated — 0.3 against 0.2
is suggestive, not settled.

**Also noted: the attribution tool's `other` bucket hid a contract violation.**
The continuation-with-two-options case fell into `other` and was only found by
reading the rejected responses by hand. `retryRate.ts` classifies three codes and
lumps the rest; that is fine for ranking but not for trusting a shift in
composition.

### `LIFE-SI-LEAN-3` — the lean SI is NOT deployable, and the reason is the interesting part

Making `lean` the default turned **43 unit tests red**, across roughly 25
invariants the rewrite silently dropped: `LIFE-OPT-VARIETY` (the spread of
action types across a grid), `LIFE-STORY-MOVE` (break a scene stalled in one
place with one person), `LIFE-LANG-13` (the player-voice paths),
`LIFE-SPEECH-1/3/4`, `LIFE-EX-PLAN-5`, `LIFE-INTRO-OPT`, the scene turn window,
`sceneProgress` every turn, canon self-closing, the figurative-line cap,
`LIFE-NPC-MEM-1`, `LIFE-STORY-RHYTHM`, `LIFE-STORY-STAGE-1`.

**Several of those encode the owner's own past complaints** — *"the same options
every time"*, scenes that sit in one room with one person. Shipping the lean SI
would most likely regress exactly the reports it was built after.

**This does not retract the lean measurement**, which stands: retry 18.6% → 3.0%
with disjoint intervals, 0 unparseable in 99 calls, 60% fewer input tokens,
output length unchanged. **It bounds what that measurement covers.** The retry
rate and the parse rate see only what the VALIDATOR and the PARSER check. The 43
tests cover rules neither one checks — which is precisely the class the owner
reports by hand and no automated gate has ever caught.

**The lesson, and it generalises past this file:** "no test went red" was true
of the lean SI only because the harness measures contract, not content. A prompt
can lose a quarter of its rules, keep a clean parse rate, improve its retry rate,
and still be worse — and the only instrument that would have said so is the 43
assertions I did not run until I tried to make it the default.

The variant stays behind `setStepSiVariant('lean')` as WORK IN PROGRESS: port the
dropped rules back (it grows past 19k, which is fine — the target was never a
number), then re-measure both the retry rate AND the 43.

### `LIFE-CONFORM-1` — the 43, rewritten as assertions on OUTPUT

`tests/playthrough/ruleConformance.ts`. Named predicates over parsed `life_step`
archives, each returning a hit/total, compared between two arms with Wilson
intervals. Offline, free, and it answers the question the 43 were only ever
gesturing at: **does the model still DO this.**

**A string test cannot fail informatively in either direction.** The 43 went red
on a rewrite without saying which behaviours had actually stopped, and
`LIFE-NPC-MEM-1`'s phrase sits in the shipped SI while its behaviour runs at
18–22%. That test passes. It has always passed.

**`LIFE-SI-LEAN-4` said the lean arm showed "no mechanically checkable
regression". That conclusion is SUPERSEDED and it was wrong.** It was not wrong
about the data; it was wrong about the rules it checked — chiefly *"options carry
`speechAct`"*, which measured compliance with the OPPOSITE of the rule
(`stepSpeech.ts` instructs the model to OMIT `speechAct` for the default `act`).
Rules derived from the 43 instead of invented alongside them find four disjoint
regressions, full (n=96 turns) vs lean (n=192):

| rule | full | lean | |
|---|---|---|---|
| `LIFE-SPEECH-3` say option carries `gradeTarget` | 12% [7, 20] | **0%** [0, 3] | 0 of 130 |
| `LIFE-SPEECH-3` no fill-blank NODE after a `say` pick | 97% [86, 100] | **75%** [61, 85] | |
| `LIFE-NODE-7` turn offers a choice (not a continuation) | 99% [94, 100] | **76%** [70, 82] | |
| `LIFE-OPT-VARIETY` grid mixes action types | 100% [96, 100] | **90%** [84, 94] | |

**The `gradeTarget` row is the whole argument about prompt bloat, settled.** The
lean SI still carries the FIELD — one accurate line in the schema block, *"set it
to GRADE this pick"*. The full SI carries the field plus a section: when to use
it, the ~1-in-3 cadence, the level ceiling, no cognates, fresh targets. The field
alone yields **zero uses in 319 turns**; the section yields 12%. `gradeTarget` is
the only route by which a player ever PRODUCES language, so the lean arm is a
version of the app in which that never happens — and the retry rate, the parse
rate and all 43 string tests are equally blind to it.

The `say`-pick row compounds it: the lean arm doesn't merely stop grading speech,
it grades it in the form the rule closes — a fill-blank NODE authored after the
line has already been spoken, which is unauthorable by construction.

**Two defects found in the checker before its numbers were believed**, both of
which produced confident wrong readings first:

- **A retry does not share its turn's `seq`.** Deduping on `seq` is a silent
  no-op, so every REJECTED attempt counted as a turn the player saw — a 62%
  lead-in omission rate against a validator that rejects exactly that. A turn is
  the run from an `attempt: 0` up to the next one.
- **`\b` is ASCII.** `/\bétouffante\b/` does not match `l'air étouffante`, so
  every accented French target read as "no leak" — a check that cannot fire
  reports 100%. Third appearance of this bug in this harness; `containsWord` uses
  `\p{L}` lookarounds.

**What it does NOT cover, and this is the whole point of writing it down.**
Roughly a dozen of the 43 are not mechanically checkable: the figurative-line
cap, establish→converse→bridge rhythm, whether a scene has stalled, gentle
introductions, prose register, whether a name was reached for as mood.
`bannedLexicon.ts` makes the same admission about its own noun list — *"a ship's
deck really is salted"* — so only the IDIOM list is counted here. Those need a
judge (±0.2, `LIFE-BENCH-7`) or a human reading transcripts. Nothing in this file
covers them, and treating a green table as coverage is the exact error that let
25 invariants go missing.

**Also surfaced, in both arms, so not a lean regression:** `hear` options are
effectively never authored — 0 of 271 full-arm options, 1 of 325 lean. Inbound
comprehension still happens (it is ~40% of question nodes), but it is not reached
through the `hear` direction the speech machinery is built around, which leaves
`LIFE-SPEECH-4` with nothing to check.

### `LIFE-CONFORM-2` — the unmeasurable dozen, read by hand

`LIFE-CONFORM-1` covers what a predicate can see. The rest of the 43 were read:
three full transcripts (one full-arm, two lean), turn by turn, both languages.
The judge cannot arbitrate this — full 2.41 vs lean 2.34 composite, against a
run-to-run spread of ~0.2 (`LIFE-BENCH-7`) — so what follows is my reading,
labelled as such, with the lines quoted so it can be disagreed with.

**Worse in the lean arm:**

- **Gentle introductions are gone.** Full: *"Gaël Vran — l'homme qui t'apprend le
  métier — frappe sur le bois de ton établi"*, the appositive doing the work the
  rule asks for. Lean: Beñat, Elur Vaz, Miren Sol and Zorion each arrive already
  speaking, with nothing saying who they are. Scene 1 opens *"Miren ajuste ses
  lunettes"* and scene 2 *"Zorion est assis sur son tabouret"* — a name and a
  posture, no relationship, in a language the player is still learning.
- **Story rhythm collapses into narration.** Full scene 0 establishes, then
  converses with Yannick across six turns, then bridges. Lean scene 0 sends the
  player ALONE to the attic for turns 3-6; lean scene 2 opens with three
  consecutive narrator-only turns of an old man fidgeting with a satchel. The
  continuation rate (99% → 76%) understates this because continuations CLUSTER —
  they are not spread evenly, they arrive two and three in a row, and a run of
  them is a cutscene.
- **The scene drifts off its declared subject.** Lean scene 2 declares subject
  *"Clearing an obstruction in the passage"* and openQuestion *"Will the spill of
  flour across the thoroughfare be cleaned before the watch patrol arrives?"* —
  and then delivers eight turns about a sealed leather satchel. Flour is never
  mentioned. Full scene 2 declares *"a dispute over missing floor sweepings"* and
  delivers exactly that. `LIFE-SCENE-25`'s one-subject rule is among the 43.
- **A comprehension question with its own answer above it**, 5 of 37 in lean
  against 0 of 16 in full (intervals overlap — observed, not shown). Lean, one
  turn: beat *"Ces conduits sont fragiles, Alice. Il nous faut un plan B si le
  métal cède sous notre poids."* then the question, testing that line verbatim.
  Now a rule (`LIFE-CONTRACT-EX-6 comprehension does not show the tested line
  first`).

**No difference I can see:**

- **The figurative-line cap and prose register.** Both arms run at about one
  figurative line per turn and neither is purple — lean's *"libérant une odeur de
  soufre et de vieille encre"* and full's *"la brume matinale s'accroche encore
  aux anfractuosités des vieux murs"* are each one image, doing concrete work.
  This is a rule I expected to see fail on the lean arm and it does not.
- **Difficulty gating on tested targets.** At difficulty 3 both arms stay inside
  the band (*"Le ressort est vraiment vieux"*, *"Le plan est presque fini"*).

**Standing defects in BOTH arms — not lean regressions, and worth fixing:**

- **An NPC bubble containing third-person narration about its own speaker**,
  ~7-8% of npc beats in both. Full: *"Paol serre le paquet contre lui avec une
  expression de panique soudaine"* under Paol's avatar. Lean: *"Miren → Elle
  fouille dans son rouleau de velours et en sort une petite scie diamantée"*.
  That is a narrator beat wearing a character's face, and `LIFE-CONTRACT-BUBBLE`
  says a bubble holds the verbatim utterance and nothing else. **Deliberately NOT
  encoded as a rule:** an NPC legitimately says *"Il a raison"* or *"Elle ralentit
  souvent au troisième palier"* about someone else, and no cheap predicate
  separates those from the mislabels — every version I tried flagged both at the
  same rate in both arms. It needs the judge or a different repair.
- **`LIFE-INTRO-OPT` is violated by the FULL arm too.** Full scene 2 turn 4
  offers *"Ask Elouan what he saw"* before Elouan has appeared in a single beat.
  The rule's string test passes.
- **No scene resolves.** Every scene in every run of both arms ends *"never
  resolved — cut at the 8-turn ceiling"*. `LIFE-SCENE-END-1`'s material change is
  not happening anywhere, in either prompt.

### `LIFE-SI-LEAN-5/6` — five rules ported, measured, and SHIPPED as the default

The lean SI is now the default (`step.ts`), at **20,894 characters against the
full SI's ~73,000**. Five rules were ported back for ~1,800 characters, chosen
from `LIFE-CONFORM-1`'s four measured regressions plus one reading, and the rest
of the 43 were left out on evidence rather than on nerve.

**Final, full arm (n=96 turns) vs shipped lean (n=84):**

| rule | full | shipped lean | |
|---|---|---|---|
| `gradeTarget` on a say option | 12% [7, 20] | **16%** [9, 27] | restored |
| turn offers a choice (not a continuation) | 99% [94, 100] | **96%** [90, 99] | restored |
| grid mixes action types | 100% [96, 100] | **100%** [95, 100] | restored |
| `npcMemoryUpdates` emitted | 22% [15, 31] | **43%** [33, 54] | better, disjoint |
| no fill-blank NODE after a `say` pick | 97% [86, 100] | 75% [58, 87] | still low |
| lead-in does not leak the tested line | 90% [70, 97] | 62% [43, 78] pooled | still low |

**`LIFE-SI-LEAN-6` — the heading was the fix, and two rewrites had to fail first.**
`gradeTarget` took three attempts:

1. Stating the rule with its cadence, under the heading *"SPEECH THE PLAYER
   PRODUCES"* — **0% → 2%**.
2. Folding it into `QUESTIONS` with the option route first and a worked example
   — **back to 0%**, and three other rows moved the wrong way (all overlapping
   with attempt 1, so *not shown* to differ from it — but shown worse than full,
   which attempt 1 was not).
3. Renaming the section **`OUTBOUND GRADING`** — **16%**, above the full arm.

The cause was mechanical, not rhetorical. `stepOutputSchemaBlock` is shared with
the full SI **byte for byte**, and its `gradeTarget` line ends *"See OUTBOUND
GRADING below."* The full SI has a section by that name. The lean SI did not, and
neither did either of my rewrites — so the schema's own cross-reference pointed
at nothing and the rule dangled next to a field the model could see but had no
instruction for.

This is `LIFE-CONTRACT-EX-10`'s lesson in a second place: **when two rewrites of
a rule cannot move a number, stop rewriting and look for the mechanism.** There
it was a validator that disagreed with the prompt; here it is a pointer into a
section that does not exist. Both are invisible to every string test, because a
string test asks whether words are present, never whether they are REACHABLE.

**Left out, each on a measurement rather than a preference:**

- **The figurative-line cap, prose register, difficulty-gated prose** — three
  transcripts read side by side showed no difference (`LIFE-CONFORM-2`).
- **`LEXICON_RULES`** — `CLICHE-NAME` unmoved (2 offending names in 220 vs 3 in
  432; names come from `sagaOpen`/`worldArc`, which still carry the rule), and
  judged `CLICHE` overlaps: full 1.92 [1.75, 2.08] vs lean 1.67 [1.47, 1.86].
  `LIFE-STORY-LEXICON-2`'s idiom half runs at 100% in both arms.
- **The scene turn window** — stated twice. `renderScenePace` sends the same
  6/9/12 thresholds EVERY TURN with an escalating stage, and the lean SI keeps
  the pointer to it. The SI's copy was the duplicate.

**Two rows are still low and are not being claimed as fixed.** Both intervals
overlap the full arm's, so neither is *shown* worse, but both sit ~20 points
below it across every ported run, which is not the shape of noise. `lead-in
leaks the tested line` is repaired downstream (`stripLeakingLeadIns` drops the
beat) and `no fill-blank NODE after a say pick` is an authoring PREFERENCE whose
violation is contract-legal — `LIFE-SPEECH-1`'s projection explicitly allows
`say` → fill-blank. Neither costs the player a broken turn; both are the first
rows to look at on the next run.

### `LIFE-EX-CADENCE-6` — the two "still low" rows were one prompt block contradicting three rules

`LIFE-SI-LEAN-5/6` shipped with two rows below the full arm and flagged them for
the next run. They were not SI problems at all. `renderPracticeCadence` — the
`<practiceCadence>` block, sent EVERY graded turn — was instructing the model to
do three things the system instruction and the validator forbid. It is per-turn
and specific, so it wins.

**Measured over 12 archived runs, across BOTH SI arms** (which is why the full
arm shows the same defects):

| the instruction | what it produced |
|---|---|
| `(no beats, no top-level options)` | **25 of 25** act-pick question nodes that shipped with no lead-in came from a turn carrying this. Zero exceptions. |
| fill-blank slot: *"Take a line the player is about to SAY… not optional on this slot"* | **40 of 40** fill-blank NODES following a `say` pick landed on a `kind="fill-blank"` slot. Not one appeared anywhere else. |
| comprehension slot: *"Take an NPC line…"*, and forced: *"have someone say the thing worth testing, then test it"* | comprehension slots leaked the tested line at **23–32%**, against fill-blank's 8–18%. |

The first is the sharpest: **the prompt was instructing the model into a retry.**
`validateStep` REJECTS a question node with no beats after an action pick
(`question-omits-lead-in`), `leadInDirective` already renders the correct
conditional rule per turn, and this block answered the same question again,
unconditionally, wrongly.

**The fixes are removals and a routing, not rewordings:**

1. The shape line no longer mentions beats — it points at `<leadIn>`, which is
   conditional on the pick and agrees with the validator. "No top-level options"
   is unconditional and stays.
2. `renderPracticeCadence` now takes `pickedSpeechAct`. On a PRODUCTION slot
   after a `say` pick it routes the exercise to a `gradeTarget` on an option and
   tells the node to stay narrative — the slot keeps its purpose and changes its
   address. After any other pick the node form is unchanged.
3. The comprehension rule asks for the NPC's NEXT line and says it must not
   already be on screen; the forced escape hatch may still rescue a thin moment
   but may not pre-write the line.

**Result, 4 runs (n=78 turns), against the full arm — no row worse, two better:**

| rule | before (shipped lean) | after | full arm |
|---|---|---|---|
| no fill-blank NODE after a `say` pick | 75% [58, 87] | **100%** [86, 100] | 97% |
| question after an act pick has a lead-in | 64% [39, 84] | **100%** [83, 100] | 61% |
| lead-in does not leak the tested line | 40% [17, 69] | **89%** [69, 97] | 90% |
| `gradeTarget` on a say option | 16% [9, 27] | **22%** [13, 36] | 12% |
| comprehension does not show the line first | 80% [55, 93] | **92%** [65, 99] | 100% |
