**The lesson, third time this session and worth stating as a rule.** Two prompt
REWRITES of `gradeTarget` moved nothing (`LIFE-SI-LEAN-6`); what moved it was a
dangling cross-reference. Five rewrites of the lead-in rule moved nothing
(`LIFE-CONTRACT-EX-9/10`); what moved it was first a validator that disagreed,
and now a second instruction that contradicted. **When a rule is present,
well-worded, and disobeyed, stop editing the rule.** It is one of:

- **unreachable** — the text is there but nothing points at it (`LIFE-SI-LEAN-6`);
- **contradicted** — another instruction, usually a more specific per-turn one, says the opposite (this invariant);
- **disagreed with by code** — a validator or repair enforces something else (`LIFE-CONTRACT-EX-10`).

None of the three is visible to a substring test, because all three are about
whether a rule is REACHABLE and CONSISTENT, not whether its words are present.
That is what `ruleConformance.ts` is for, and all three were found by pointing it
at archives and asking which per-turn tag produced each violation.

### `LIFE-EX-CADENCE-7` — production gets its own clock, and the tier-none block stops switching it off

Owner, 2026-08-10: *"I'm definitely finding the model is missing most
opportunities to test me saying something… Maybe gate only on if I've had
specifically a generate based question recently."* Right, and there was a second,
larger mechanism sitting behind it.

**Measured over 78 turns, split by the cadence tier that produced them:**

| tier | turns | `say` options offered | `gradeTarget` marked |
|---|---|---|---|
| `none` | 42 (**54% of all turns**) | 22 | **1** (4.5%) |
| `due` | 27 | 21 | 8 (38%) |
| `forced` | 9 | 6 | 2 (33%) |

The `tier="none"` block said *"do not emit kind `question` and do not author a
gradedPlan"*. **`gradeTarget` is not a gradedPlan** — it is a field on an option,
it costs no turn, it interrupts nothing and it fires only if the player picks
that option. Nothing about it needs the node cadence. The model read the
prohibition as "no grading this turn" and dropped production with it, on the
majority of all turns.

The owner's mechanism is what fed it: `cyoaStreak` resets on ANY graded moment,
so every comprehension question bought two turns at `tier="none"` — two turns of
production suppression.

**Both fixed, and the two are complementary:**

1. The tier-none block carves `gradeTarget` out explicitly.
2. `productionStreak` counts decisions since the last **fill-blank** correction
   only, and `renderProductionCadence` fires at `PRODUCTION_DUE = 3` regardless
   of tier. It asks for an OPTION, never a node — the node route is the expensive
   one and belongs to `<practiceCadence>`; asking for it here would put two
   blocks back in the business of contradicting each other
   (`LIFE-EX-CADENCE-6`).

**Result:** `gradeTarget` 22% → **74%** of say options, and at `tier="none"`
specifically 5% → 70%. What the player actually EXPERIENCES — production reached,
not merely offered — went from **one every 5.5 turns to one every 3.5**.

**Checked for the obvious overshoot, and it did not happen.** The SI warns that
*"marking every say option turns the story into a drill"*:

- **Turns marking MORE THAN ONE option: 0, before and after.** "Mark ONE and
  leave the others plain" holds exactly.
- **Question NODE density unchanged: 27% → 24%.** The extra production rides
  options, which is the whole point — it costs no turns.
- Cost: **$0.036 → $0.043 per run (+21%)**, from `life_graded_question` 33 → 53.
  That is the honest price of roughly twice the production practice, on the
  exercise the app exists for.

### `LIFE-CONTRACT-BUBBLE-5` — the one beat after a `say` pick needs a SPEAKER

**Found by reading a transcript after the change above, and it is a regression
the change AMPLIFIED rather than caused.** `renderAlreadySpoken` offered *"AT MOST
ONE more beat — the reply, or one short reaction"* and never said whose beat a
reaction is. The model has one slot, an NPC visibly reacting, and the line test
three sections away in the SI — so it wrote the narration into the NPC's bubble.
*"Padrig attrape la pièce avec un sourire large"* rendered under Padrig's avatar
as something Padrig SAID.

Rate of npc beats that are third-person narration about their own speaker:

| arm | rate |
|---|---|
| full SI | 7% [3, 16] |
| lean, before `LIFE-EX-CADENCE-7` | 14% [8, 25] |
| lean, after it | **22%** [14, 33] |
| after naming the speaker | **3%** [1, 11] |

**12 of 17 followed a `say` pick**, which is the tell: more production means more
`say` picks means more turns hitting the one-beat budget, and the budget was
silent on the distinction at the exact point the choice is made. Naming both
options' speakers — *"the reply, an npc beat carrying their words and nothing
else, OR one short reaction, which is a narrator beat, ALWAYS"* — takes it to 3%,
disjoint from 22% and better than the full SI has ever measured.

**The general form, and it is the same lesson as the last three:** the rule
existed (`LIFE-CONTRACT-BUBBLE-1`'s line test) and was reachable and
uncontradicted. What was missing was that it was not stated WHERE THE DECISION IS
MADE. A rule three sections away loses to a sentence in the turn.

### `LIFE-EX-15` — the exercise rotations were phase-locked to the kind alternation

Owner, 2026-08-10: *"making sure all exercise types are cycled through… exercise
types being gated behind levels may be one place that's actually tripping up the
prompt."* The symptom was real; the cause was a **counter collision in code**,
and the prompt was never involved — the model picks `fill-blank` vs
`comprehension` and CODE picks the presentation.

`exerciseSeed` was "graded questions resolved in this wurld" — one shared
counter. But the kind rotation (`gradedKindForSlot`, since deleted by
`LIFE-EX-18`) read the same number to choose the KIND: even →
fill-blank, odd → comprehension. So a comprehension could only ever occur at an
ODD seed, and `chooseComprehensionVariant` returns `listen` at exactly
`seed % 2 === 1`.

**`comprehension:read` was not rare, it was IMPOSSIBLE at every level ≥ 3** —
about half of all questions, with exactly one presentation. That is verbatim the
defect `chooseComprehensionVariant`'s own comment says it was written to remove
(*"Comprehension had exactly one presentation while the fill-blank had four"*).
The rotation's PHASE was locked to the alternation's parity, so it could not
rotate. The fill-blank side survived only by luck: a 3-slot rotation sampled at
even seeds still visits all three, at half rate.

**Reachable presentations, before → after:**

| level | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| 1–2 | `fill:chips`, `fill:listen`, `comp:read` | unchanged (3) |
| 3 | 4 — no `comp:read` | **5** — `comp:read` restored |
| 4+ | 4 — no `comp:read` | **5** — `comp:read` restored |

The fix is `gradedOrdinal(events, kind)`: index each kind's rotation by how many
of THAT KIND have resolved, so the two rotations advance independently of the
alternation and of each other.

**Why no test caught it, which is the transferable part.** Both functions were
correct in isolation and every existing test tested them in isolation.
`chooseComprehensionVariant(level, 1)` really does return `listen`; the bug is
that nothing ever calls it with an even number. The new tests WALK THE SEQUENCE a
player experiences — kind chosen by slot, variant chosen by ordinal — and assert
reachability, which is the only framing in which the defect exists at all.

**No single level reaches all six presentations, by design.** Level 3 reaches
five; level 4+ trades `fill:chips` for `fill:type` because typing REPLACES the
multiple-choice scaffold rather than joining it. **Level 3 is the level to test
at** — pinned as a test so it stops being folklore.

### `LIFE-EX-16` — the grading band is dynamic on the player's level

The owner's instinct that the prompt should be level-dynamic was right, just not
about presentations. `OUTBOUND GRADING` said a `gradeTarget` must be *"at the
player's level"* and never said what that meant at THIS level.
`gradeTargetBand(difficulty)` — which already existed, was tested, and was
orphaned when the full SI went — renders only the band the player is on:
level ≤ 2 gets the concrete-word floor plus the *"never grade `voudrais` here"*
counter-example, ≤ 4 gets everyday grammar, above that the subjunctive and idiom.
One band, not three, which is the `LIFE-LANG-9.1` cut applied in a second place.

### `LIFE-SPEECH-7` — `hear` has no forcing function, and two definitions could not give it one

**OPEN — a design fork for the owner, not a bug with a fix.**

`speechAct: "hear"` (the pick where the player RECEIVES a line) is effectively
never authored: **4 of 2,110** archived options across every measured arm.

**It does not merely idle, it misfires.** All four were AMBIENT SOUND — *"Listen
at the door for guard activity"*, *"Listen for the delivery carriage"*, *"Listen
for footsteps"* — which is `hear` in its ordinary English sense (perceive with
the ears) and the wrong one for a contract that means "a line of language arrives
that can be comprehension-tested". A fair reading of a field called `hear`.
Downstream: **2 of the 4 picks produced a fill-blank node**, and
`gradedPlanDirectionFix` converted it to comprehension because the projection
said a `hear` pick constrains the node — a repair that moved the line the PLAYER
was going to say into an NPC's mouth. **That path is now CLOSED** (`LIFE-EX-18`
deleted the projection), which changes what the fork below is deciding: it is no
longer "stop the damage", it is purely "is player-chosen comprehension worth
building".

**Two definitions have now failed to move it.** The deleted full SI defined it
richly (*"the pick has the player RECEIVE an NPC's content line, or READ
something"*) → 0. A positive definition with the ambient counter-example drawn
from the actual failures, added and measured over 4 runs → **0 of 209**, and
reverted for earning nothing.

**The mechanism, which is why no third wording will work either: `hear` is the
only speechAct nothing ever asks for.**

| speechAct | what demands it |
|---|---|
| `say` | `<productionCadence>`, `OUTBOUND GRADING`, and `saysLine` is schema-REQUIRED on it |
| `act` | the default — every option that omits `speechAct` is one |
| `hear` | **nothing.** `requiredTypeForTurn` rotates ACTION types (`do`/`move`/`observe`/`offer`); `speechAct` is orthogonal and unrotated |

Same shape as `gradeTarget` before `LIFE-EX-CADENCE-7`: a thing the model MAY do,
competing for attention against things actively demanded, with no slot of its own.

**The fork:**

1. **Give it a forcing function** — a rotation slot or a cadence block, the way
   production got one. What it buys is player-chosen comprehension: *"Let her
   explain"* vs *"Cut her off"* is a real story choice, and today the player
   never gets it — comprehension arrives ~half the time, always scheduled, never
   chosen. This is a story-design addition with consequences, so it is the
   owner's call.
2. **Delete `hear` from the contract.** Comprehension already happens without it.
   Removing it simplifies `optionSpeechActIssues` and drops a field authored
   4 times in 2,110 options, every one of them wrongly.

### `LIFE-SPEECH-8` — the fork, resolved: `hear` is DELETED

Owner, 2026-08-10, delegating the call. **Option 2.** The enum is `'say' | 'act'`.

**The argument that decided it was not "the field is dead".** A dead field is
cheap to leave. What the audit found is that `hear` is not inert — it is wired
to exactly one live behaviour, and that behaviour is backwards:

> `LIFE-CONTRACT-EX-7` requires a question node following an ACTION pick to open
> with a lead-in beat narrating what the player did. `ValidatorContext` DISARMS
> that requirement when the pick was `hear`, on the reasoning that *"a `hear`
> pick means the NPC speaking IS the event"*.

That reasoning is sound for what `hear` was designed to mean. It is wrong for
what `hear` is actually used for. **All 4 real uses were ambient sound** —
*"Listen at the door for guard activity"*, *"Listen for the delivery carriage"*,
*"Listen for footsteps"* — and every one of those is a player ACTION with a
physical outcome the next node owes the player a beat about. So the disarm was
suppressing the lead-in on precisely the turns that needed one.

Deleting `hear` re-arms it. Those picks become `act` (or absent), the validator
requires the beat, and the player is told what they heard instead of being
dropped into a question about it. **This is a bug fix, not only a simplification
— which is why "leave it, it's harmless" was the wrong read.**

**Nothing else reads it.** The two live reads of `pickedSpeechAct` are
`=== 'act'` (the lead-in arm, above) and `=== 'say'` (the production route,
`LIFE-EX-18`). `hear` participates in neither except by falling into the
not-`act` bucket alongside `null`. The direction projection that used to consume
it went with `LIFE-EX-18`.

**Why the `hear` semantics were never recoverable by wording.** Two definitions
failed (0, then 0 of 209) because the failure is not lexical: `hear` in ordinary
English means *perceive with the ears*, the contract needed it to mean *a line of
language arrives that can be comprehension-tested*, and the model reads English.
A third wording would have failed the same way. The field name was fighting the
contract.

### `LIFE-SPEECH-9` — player-CHOSEN comprehension (PROPOSED, not built)

Kept out of the deletion above so the idea is not lost with the field.

Comprehension today is always SCHEDULED — the cadence decides a turn is graded
and the model picks a line. The player never chooses to listen. *"Let her
explain"* vs *"Cut her off"* is a real story decision with a real pedagogical
consequence, and it does not exist.

**This does not need `hear` to survive in the meantime, and that is the whole
reason deleting was safe.** The feature IS the forcing function — a rotation slot
or a cadence block that demands the option, the way `LIFE-EX-CADENCE-7` demands
`gradeTarget`. The enum value is one line of the change, added back when the
machinery that asks for it is written. Keeping a field with no demand does not
make the demand cheaper to build later; it just leaves something for the next
person to wire up by mistake, which is how it survived this long.

**Revisit when:** someone wants player-directed pacing of the graded stream. The
`LIFE-EX-CADENCE-7` pattern (own counter, own block, asks for an OPTION not a
node) is the template.

### `LIFE-EX-18` — the graded KIND is DERIVED from whose line it is, not declared

**LANDED** (the derivation + the retirements; the `fill-blank` → `generation`
rename is deliberately deferred — see *What shipped* at the end of this section).
Owner, 2026-08-10:
*"No need for the model to even output if it's comprehension or generation. If
the npc id is me then it's a generation… If it's another npc then any
comprehension exercise."*

**The discriminant is already redundant.** A `fill-blank` plan carries
`sentenceContext` — the line the PLAYER is about to say. A `comprehension` plan
carries `npcUtterance` + `speakerName` + `npcId` — an NPC's line. The `kind` is
fully determined by whose line it is, so the model is being asked to keep a
label consistent with the fields beside it, and `gradedPlanDirectionFix` exists
purely to repair the cases where it doesn't.

**What the model would author instead:** a LINE, and WHO SAYS IT. Code derives:

| speaker | category | presentation |
|---|---|---|
| the player | generation | any the level permits (`fillBlankVariantsForLevel`) |
| an NPC | comprehension | `read` / `listen` by level |
| absent | comprehension | `read` — there is no voice to speak it |

**The objection, and why it no longer holds.** `LIFE-EX-CADENCE-4` introduced the
kind ROTATION because *"left to choose, the model picked comprehension both times
and production never fired once"*. Deriving the kind hands that choice back. But
that was written when the NODE was the only production route. Measured now, over
190 turns:

| production route | count |
|---|---|
| `gradeTarget` on an option | **112** (64% of 175 say options) |
| fill-blank NODE | **11** — and all 11 had no speech pick behind them, i.e. the legitimate exception |

**Production is 91% option-route, driven by its own clock** (`LIFE-EX-CADENCE-7`).
The node rotation is no longer what makes production happen — and it is actively
fighting `LIFE-SPEECH-3`, which tells the model NOT to author a fill-blank node
after a speech pick. `LIFE-EX-CADENCE-6` had to add a routing rule to settle that
fight (75% → 100%); deriving the kind removes the fight instead of refereeing it.

**What retires with the discriminant:** `gradedPlanDirectionFix`, the
`LIFE-SPEECH-4` say/hear→kind projection (and with it the `hear` misfire in
`LIFE-SPEECH-7`), and `gradedKindForSlot`. **What does NOT retire:** the cadence's
*whether* — streak, tier, `PRODUCTION_DUE`. Only the *which kind* half goes.

**One measured caveat.** The speaker field becomes load-bearing for the exercise,
not just the avatar. Over 198 archived comprehension plans, **12% carry no
`npcId` and 4% no `speakerName`** — and sampling those, they are NOT signs or
notes (the owner's guess): they are ordinary dialogue with a field omitted.
*"Hé, qu'est-ce que vous faites ici à cette heure, citoyenne ?"* has a speaker;
the model just didn't name them. So the absent-speaker branch is a graceful
FALLBACK for an incomplete field rather than a sign-reading feature, and its cost
is that ~12% of comprehensions would never reach the `listen` variant. That is a
safe failure (read is always valid) and it is consistent with what already
happens to the audio, since TTS needs the same field to pick a voice.

**Naming.** With the discriminant gone the categories are only ever spoken of in
code, so they should be named for what they are: **generation** and
**comprehension**. `fill-blank` — a presentation name standing in for a category
— disappears with the field.

> **SUPERSEDED by `LIFE-EX-19`.** The premise above is false: the discriminant is
> NOT gone — `kind` was kept in the SI (see *What shipped*), so `fill-blank` is
> still a value the model writes. The rename happened anyway, but LAYERED rather
> than global, for the reason `LIFE-EX-19` gives.

### `LIFE-EX-19` — the rename is LAYERED: `fill-blank` on the wire, `generation` in code

Scoped from a survey, not from the estimate. The estimate in `LIFE-EX-18` was
"69 sites, 24 files"; the real surface is **593 occurrences across 76 files**,
and two findings from that survey reshaped the job.

**1. `fill-blank` in `src/lib/practiceGenerator/` is a DIFFERENT FEATURE.** The
SM-2 flashcard practice screen has its own `fill-blank` question type, sharing
the string and nothing else — no imports, no shared type, only two "mirrors the
pattern" comments. There it is a presentation name for a genuine fill-in-the-
blank, which is correct and stays. **Out of scope, and renaming it would have
been a straightforward mistake.**

**2. The two layers want different words, so they get different words.**

| layer | name | why |
|---|---|---|
| what the MODEL authors | `fill-blank` | it writes `sentenceContext` + `testTarget` — a sentence and the word to blank out. The name describes the task concretely. |
| what the CODE reasons about | `generation` | the pedagogical category "the player produces language", whose presentations are chips · type · order · listen. |

`fillBlankVariantsForLevel` returning four variants of which exactly ONE is a
fill-blank is the misleading name that prompted this, and it is a code-side
name. The SI's `"kind": "fill-blank"` is not misleading at all — it tells the
model what to write.

**Why the wire keeps the old name.** Renaming it would be an unmeasured prompt
change for zero behaviour, and `generation` is the more ABSTRACT of the two
words — which is the `LIFE-SPEECH-8` failure mode in reverse (`hear` failed
because the word fought what the model had to do). If someone later wants one
name everywhere, it is a one-line change at the seam below, and it is
measurable. Do it with a measurement or not at all.

**The seam already exists.** `ZGradedPlan`'s `z.preprocess` (`LIFE-EX-18`) is
the single wire→internal translation point: it maps a declared `fill-blank` and
a field-derived generation alike onto `'generation'`, so everything downstream of
the parse sees one name. Stored `payload.kind` is the internal name.

#### What shipped

`deriveGradedKind(plan)` in `stepSchema.ts` reads the two mutually-exclusive
line fields and returns the kind; `ZGradedPlan` is a `z.preprocess` in front of
the discriminated union that injects it. A declared kind that AGREES is left
alone, one that DISAGREES is overwritten — **the fields cannot be wrong about
whose line they hold.**

Retired with it: `gradedPlanDirectionFix`, `repairGradedPlan`,
`gradedKindForSlot` / `gradedSlotIndex` / `GradedKind`, and the `kind="…"`
attribute on `<practiceCadence>`.

**The `kind` field STAYS in the SI's output schema, and that is a reversal.** The
plan was to drop it — it is derived, so asking for it looks like waste. Two
things say otherwise. It is the TIE-BREAK: `deriveGradedKind` returns `null` when
both line fields are present or neither is, and in the both case the declaration
picks the arm and Zod strips the stray field, where dropping it would make the
plan unparseable and spend a retry. And there is no measurement saying removal
helps — the field costs ~2 tokens per shape, and *"a rule earns its characters"*
cuts both ways: it is not licence to delete on an aesthetic.

**The near-miss, recorded because it is the general form of this failure.**
Deleting the kind rotation silently deleted two rules that were hanging off it,
both with measurements behind them:

| rule | was attached to | actually keys off |
|---|---|---|
| the tested line has not been said yet (leak: 23-32%) | the comprehension slot | nothing — true of both kinds |
| after a `say` pick, production rides the option (**40 of 40**) | the fill-blank slot | the PICK, a different axis |

Neither ever needed a slot kind; being attached to one is the tell that they
were bolted to the nearest available branch rather than to their own condition.
Both are restated in `renderPracticeCadence` without it — the first
unconditionally, the second gated on `pickedSpeechAct === 'say'`.

**Deleting a mechanism is allowed to delete the mechanism. It is not allowed to
quietly delete the measured rules riding on it.** The only reason this was
caught is that the tests named the RULES (`a comprehension slot never asks for a
line already on screen`) and not just the strings — a test asserting
`toContain('kind="comprehension"')` would have been deleted as stale along with
the attribute, and the leak would have come back unobserved.

#### Measured after landing — 4 runs per arm, `PLAY_SEEDS=heist`

| rule | before | after | |
|---|---|---|---|
| `gradeTarget` on a say option | 59% [48, 68] | **78% [69, 85]** | intervals DISJOINT |
| lead-in does not leak the tested line | 86% [67, 95] | 100% [82, 100] | overlap — not shown |
| turn is a question (~1 in 3 target) | 28% [20, 38] | 22% [15, 31] | overlap — not shown |
| everything else (24 rows) | — | — | overlap — not shown |

**The `gradeTarget` jump is the restored after-`say` route earning its place.**
It is the one rule in this diff that says something the model was not otherwise
told per turn, and it is the only row that moved. Nothing else in the diff was
supposed to move a rate — the change removes a corruption path — and nothing
else did.

**Two things this run did NOT show, stated because the temptation is to claim
them.** (1) The `hear`-pick corruption did not occur in either arm
(`hear→fill-blank` = 0 of 48 plans), which is expected at this sample size —
`hear` is authored ~4 times in 2,110 options — so the fix rests on the earlier
`LIFE-SPEECH-7` measurement, not on this one. (2) The question rate sits below
its ~1-in-3 target in BOTH arms and the arms do not differ, so it is not this
change's doing; resolving it needs the `BENCH-1` ruler.

**One finding that cuts against the reasoning above:** across 48 graded plans in
both arms, **0 carried both line fields and 0 carried neither.** The tie-break
that `kind` is being kept for is real in the schema but was never once exercised.
The honest summary is that `kind` now stays on the second reason alone —
removing it is unmeasured — and that if it is ever removed, this is the number to
re-check first.

**What the debug overlay stops crying wolf about:** 5 of 27 plans in the before
arm were a comprehension after a `say` pick — every one logged
`graded-direction-unrepairable` at FAILED level and shipped anyway. That is a
false failure badge on ~19% of graded turns, and `LIFE-SPEECH-4` says the
authoring was correct all along. Now silent.

**Still open:** the `fill-blank` → `generation` rename (69 sites, 24 files). It
is mechanical, touches the Firestore `payload.kind` written by every
`correction` event, and mixing it into this diff would bury the behavioural
change in a rename. Separate pass.

### `LIFE-EX-20` — the listen axis is ORTHOGONAL to the response axis

`listen` was competing for a slot with `chips`, `type` and `order`, which are
not the same kind of thing at all:

| axis | question it answers | values |
|---|---|---|
| **arrival** | does the prompt reach the player as text or as SOUND? | `read` · `listen` |
| **response** | how does the player answer? | `chips` · `type` · `order` |

Putting them in one list made **`listen` mean "spoken, answered with chips"** and
made `listen`+`type` and `listen`+`order` unreachable — the two hardest and most
useful generation exercises there are (hear a sentence, then produce the missing
word from memory, or rebuild the word order from what you heard). Neither was a
decision anyone took; they fell out of the encoding, which is the `LIFE-EX-15`
failure in a different costume.

**Two lists, indexed as an ODOMETER.** The arrival axis turns on every question;
the response axis advances once per full turn of the arrival axis:

```
arrival  = arrivals[ seed % arrivals.length ]
response = responses[ floor(seed / arrivals.length) % responses.length ]
```

Not two independent `% n` on the same seed — that is exactly the phase-lock that
made `comprehension:read` unreachable (`LIFE-EX-15`). An odometer visits every
PAIR before repeating, which is the property that matters and the one a test can
assert directly.

**Reachable combinations by level**, up from the old single list:

| level | arrivals | responses | pairs | was |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | read · listen | chips | 2 | 2 |
| 2-3 | read · listen | chips · order | 4 | 3 |
| 4 | read · listen | chips · order · type | 6 | 4 |
| 5-7 | read · listen | order · type | 4 | 3 |

The level gates are unchanged (`LIFE-EX-17`); only the axis they sit on moves.
Whole-sentence COMPREHENSION listening keeps its own separate floor of 3
(`LIFE-EX-14`) — that is a different exercise, not this arrival axis, and the
distinction is the one the owner drew explicitly.

## Direction, consequence and standing — `LIFE-GOAL-*` / `LIFE-NEED-*` / `LIFE-REP-*`

**PROPOSED — the contract, not yet implemented.** Owner, 2026-08-10, after
playing a scene: *"I was asked to find a key, I saw it but picked a different
option on that turn — the key never came back up again, until suddenly it seems
like the scene is about something else. I honestly couldn't tell what I was
supposed to do or what would happen if I didn't do it. Sometimes characters
seemed mad at me sometimes they seemed my friends."* Plus the constraint that
governs all of it: *"I should still be free to make the wrong choices though and
suffer the consequences."*

### `LIFE-GOAL-0` — the measurement that starts this

Four symptoms, and three of them are one missing object. **v1 already has a
goals feature and it is DEAD.** `ZLifeWorld.currentGoals`, `worldDelta.addGoals`
/ `removeGoals`, the merge in `commitNode`, and a `<goals>` block in the prompt
all exist and are wired end to end. Measured across 4 runs / 95 turns:

| | |
|---|---|
| turns adding a goal | **0** |
| turns removing a goal | **0** |
| distinct goals ever live in the prompt | **0** |

`stepSchemaBlock` offers `"addGoals": ["..."]` and **nothing anywhere tells the
model when to use it**. This is `LIFE-SPEECH-8`'s failure one level up: a field
with no forcing function is authored zero times, and no wording fixes that — a
mechanism has to ASK. So there is no persistent objective in the game at all.
The key was a chip on one turn, minted fresh, and when the player did not take
it nothing recorded that it had ever mattered.

The fourth symptom — characters warm then cold — is a separate hole: nothing
accumulates how the player has treated anyone.

### `LIFE-GOAL-1` — the goal LADDER, not a deadline

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| **LIFE-GOAL-1** | A saga carries exactly ONE distant **saga goal**, stated and dated, known from the opening. Horizon ~20 scenes. It is the thing the story is finally about, and it is never the thing the player is doing right now. |
| **LIFE-GOAL-2** | At any moment exactly ONE **objective** is live: a concrete near thing, reachable in **1-3 scenes**. Completing it mints the next. This is where progress is FELT. |
| **LIFE-GOAL-3** | An objective is **never silently dropped**. It ends by being completed, or by the world closing it in the fiction with a reason the player sees. Not taking the option that would advance it changes nothing about whether it is still live. |
| **LIFE-GOAL-4** | Code owns the ladder. The model may propose the next objective when the current one closes; it may never delete one, re-date the saga goal, or state a countdown. |

**Why a ladder and not v2's single deadline.** Owner: *"a deadline should be
maybe a few scenes. Although smaller deadlines can be easier and quicker to
attain building to the main deadline that may not occur until 20 scenes later."*
That is a better design than the branch's and it fixes the branch's own worst
finding. v2 measured both ends of this and could not have both at once:

- `ENGINE2-DEADLINE-5`: every run that **reached** its deadline inside a sitting
  scored FUN 2 — the last turns become wind-down. The clock must OUTLAST a
  sitting.
- `ENGINE2-B.0`: but then the payoff *"pays out once, twelve days away, which in
  a sitting-length window is never"*, and the ledger is write-only for a
  fortnight. That is what left AGENCY at 2/5.

A ladder is the only shape that satisfies both: the near objective pays out
inside the sitting, the saga goal outlasts it. **This is NOT v2's rejected
"two deadlines" (`ENGINE2-A5`)** — that warned against two *competing* clocks
splitting the player's attention. Here the objective is a STEP toward the saga
goal, so there is one direction with milestones on it.

### `LIFE-GOAL-5` — where it is SHOWN, and where it is not

Owner's call, and it is deliberately lighter than v2's permanent ribbon.

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| **LIFE-GOAL-5** | The **opening modal** states the saga goal and the first objective. Once, at world open, on the surface the player is already reading. |
| **LIFE-GOAL-6** | During play the ONLY persistent chrome is a single **icon with a count** — `12 days`. Tapping it opens a sheet listing the saga goal, the live objective, and what has been completed. No banner, no permanent goal text. |
| **LIFE-GOAL-7** | The count is denominated in the FICTION's time and driven by the clock v1 already has (`LIFE-TIME-1`'s `timeAdvance`). No second clock, and the model still may never state a date — code owns it and only moves it forward. |
| **LIFE-GOAL-8** | The live objective is **restated at every scene close**, in words, on `SceneCompleteScreen`. |

**`LIFE-GOAL-8` is load-bearing, not decoration.** A bare count is meaningless
if the player has forgotten what it counts, and hiding the goal behind a tap
makes that likely — which is a real cost of choosing the icon over a banner. The
scene-close screen already fires every scene and is already a reading surface,
so it is where the goal gets re-anchored for free. Without it `LIFE-GOAL-6` is
one tap away from the symptom this whole section exists to fix.

The reason the icon wins anyway: v1's chrome stance keeps mechanics out of the
story (the location pill, the eye toggle, the ⋯ menu), and v2's own `C6` warns
that a second countable thing on screen is *"what made v1 unreadable"*.

### `LIFE-NEED-*` — what is in the way, said before you choose

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| **LIFE-NEED-1** | An objective MAY declare ONE need: a **person** (at a reputation floor), a **thing** that must be held, or a **place** the player must be in. Most objectives have one; some have none, and a free push is part of the rhythm. |
| **LIFE-NEED-2** | An option that would advance an objective with an unmet need is **exposed**. Resolved **in code from state** — never rolled, never a model judgement, same state → same answer in a replay and in a test. |
| **LIFE-NEED-3** | Exposure is shown **BEFORE the choice**, named and attributed, **in fiction**: *"Hélène doesn't know your work yet"*, *"the canvas is still unframed"*. Never a number, never the word "risky". An unsignalled failure is randomness; a signalled one is a decision. |
| **LIFE-NEED-4** | Taking an exposed option **still costs the turn and still moves the story** — it reveals something, or someone reacts. "Nothing happens" is a contract fault, not an outcome. |
| **LIFE-NEED-5** | **The clearing move is ALWAYS on the same grid as the block.** Information without an affordance is being told off. This is the load-bearing one: a player who can be blocked with no move that clears it has met a wall. |
| **LIFE-NEED-3.1** | **The block is rendered ABOVE the grid, and the door is a CLIENT-SYNTHESISED chip on it.** Landed 2026-08-11; until then `exposureReason` / `clearingMove` were computed and rendered nowhere, so an objective could be blocked and the grid said nothing — the same "I couldn't tell what I was supposed to do" the ladder exists to answer. `goalPrompt` was meanwhile telling the model the player saw the block *"word for word, under the option that would advance it"*, which was a promise the UI broke; that wording is now corrected to "above their options". **Why above the grid and not under one chip:** code cannot know WHICH option advances the objective — only the model can, and asking it to tag one is the shape of field this codebase has twice MEASURED being authored essentially never (`hear`, 4 of 2,110 options; `addGoals`, 0 of 95 turns). A tag that is usually absent puts the block under nothing, which is worse than over everything. It is also literally true: the OBJECTIVE is blocked, not one chip, and every chip is being picked in that light. **Why the door is synthesised in code (`CLEAR_BLOCK_ID`) rather than requested:** `LIFE-NEED-5` says the door is ALWAYS on the grid, and a door the model is merely asked for is a door that is sometimes missing — the wall the invariant forbids. Synthesising it makes the guarantee structural. It is APPENDED, never substituted, because `LIFE-NEED-4` keeps pushing at the block a real option; and it routes as free text, not through `pick()`, since no option with that id exists in the model's grid. `objectiveBlockFor` returns the reason and the door as ONE object so the grid cannot render half a block. **Two things this cost:** the `useMemo` first used to compute it sat below `LifeScreen`'s three early returns and tore the tree down on every blocked world — caught by the E2E as "Something went wrong", invisible to the unit suite, and now a plain call; and the chip needed sentence-casing at the render site (the intent text submitted to the world stays the bare phrase) or it read as a different kind of thing beside "Make breakfast". |
| **LIFE-NEED-6** | A need that can never be met must never be minted. Every named person is reachable, every named thing obtainable, every named place enterable — checked when the objective is created, not when it blocks. |

**Why the need lives on the OBJECTIVE and not in the room.** v2 tried the room
version first (`blockerOf`: the coldest person present blocks you) and deleted
it — it blocked on **proximity rather than relevance**, so whoever happened to be
standing there stopped you whatever you were reaching for. Measured: every
judged run that scored AGENCY 2 quoted the resulting generic line as its
evidence; the runs that scored 3 quoted needs.

**And why this, rather than the deadline, is the agency fix.** v2's deadline
alone left AGENCY at **2/5 on every run**, including runs that passed the FUN
gate. Their one-sentence diagnosis: *every option succeeded, so the grid was
three ways to win differing only in price, and a trade-off you always win is not
a decision.* Scarcity was necessary and not sufficient. What moved it was an
option that can fail on its own terms, with its door beside it.

### `LIFE-REP-*` — standing is reputation

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| **LIFE-REP-1** | `standing` is **reputation**: how much weight the player's word carries with this person. **Zero means "you are nobody to them yet"**, not "neutral". Below zero is a debt of regard — being disregarded, never disliked. |
| **LIFE-REP-2** | Seeded from the **role gap**, never from anything the player has done. A stringer and an editor-in-chief do not start level and neither has wronged the other. Never below −1 at open. |
| **LIFE-REP-3** | After writing the beat the model reports `felt: { who, delta: -1 \| 0 \| +1 }` — a local judgement about one person's reaction, which models are reliably good at. Code clamps to ±1 per turn and owns the running total. |
| **LIFE-REP-4** | **`0` is the normal answer.** Most turns move nothing. |
| **LIFE-REP-5** | Investing a turn in a person ALWAYS lands and always buys exactly one step. Earning regard is the reliable move; what it costs is time. |
| **LIFE-REP-5.1** | **The clearing turn's step is applied by CODE, prepended to `felt`.** Landed 2026-08-11 with `LIFE-NEED-3.1`. `LIFE-REP-5` promises the reliable move is reliable — *"Investing a turn in a person ALWAYS lands and always buys exactly one step"* — and until this, the step depended entirely on the model choosing to report a `felt` reaction for that person. That is the one field it must not depend on: `hear` (4 of 2,110) and `addGoals` (0 of 95) are this repo's two measurements of what "the model will usually mention it" is worth, and here it would silently break the one door the player was TOLD would work. So `objectiveBlockFor` carries `investIn` (the person's name, on `who` blocks only — `what` / `where` are not relationships), the clearing chip passes it through `submitFreeText` → `CommitAction` → `applyTurnSignals`, and code prepends `{ who, delta: +1 }` to the turn's `felt` list. **PREPENDED, not appended, and that ordering is the invariant:** `applyFelt` keeps the FIRST entry per person and drops the rest, so (a) a model reaction for the same person cannot stack a second step on top — `LIFE-REP-5`'s "exactly one step" — and (b) a *hostile* model reaction cannot cancel the turn the player just spent. An `investIn` naming somebody outside the cast is dropped, never minted, on the same grounds `LIFE-NEED-6` gives. **`LIFE-REP-7` is unweakened and now has a test:** the practice-outcome branch of `commitNode` carries neither `investIn` nor `felt`, so a graded answer cannot reach the standing write by any path. |
| **LIFE-REP-6** | Standing is **never a number on screen during play**. It surfaces at scene close, in words, **with the reason** — *"Bébert stopped returning your greeting after you went round him to the landlord"*. A number invites farming and turns people into a resource; a reason is what the player still remembers three scenes later when it bites. |
| **LIFE-REP-7** | **A practice answer NEVER moves standing.** Not right, not wrong, not free-text. `LIFE-CONTRACT-EX-3`'s non-mutation is unweakened and this is not negotiable: attaching social cost to a grammar slip attacks the exact behaviour the app exists to create. Consequence attaches to the CHOICE. |

**`LIFE-REP-1` is the highest-leverage line here and it is a reframe, not a
relabel.** It moves the zero point. Under the old reading only a NEGATIVE
standing blocked anything, so a world where nobody disliked the player had
nothing in the way — measured on v2's gallery fixture: the block never fired in
a single one of fifteen 24-turn runs and AGENCY came back 2 in fourteen of them.
Nobody has to dislike you for the gallery owner to not put your work up yet.

### `LIFE-CONSEQ-1` — the warned consequence

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| **LIFE-CONSEQ-1** | A pressure the player has **seen named** and left unattended for a whole scene gets **worse**. A pressure they have never been shown NEVER does. |
| **LIFE-CONSEQ-2** | Reaching a goal badly is an outcome with a story after it, never a dead end and never a game-over. |

This is the direct answer to *"I should still be free to make the wrong choices
and suffer the consequences"*. The warning is the **precondition** for the cost,
which is what separates a consequence from a gotcha — and it is why hidden
pressure may never do the same thing. Unseen pressure is weather.

**One thing v2 measured here that we must not repeat:** making pressure tick
harder across the board was tried and came back **worse** — FUN 2, PROGRESSION
2, would-return 1/3, *"a wall arriving by the back door"*. The cost lands on the
one thing the player was warned about, not on everything.

### `LIFE-GOAL-11` — the ladder narrates itself, and the CLOSE rules on the objective

**Reported 2026-08-11, from a live transcript:** *"the immediate goal didn't
regenerate in scene 2."* Two findings, one shipped and one still open.

**Shipped — the ladder was the only silent mechanic.** `SCENE-GEN`,
`SCENE-CLOSE`, `STEP`, `WORLD-ARC` and `NPC-AVATAR` all narrate themselves in
the debug log; the goal ladder emitted nothing, so a nine-minute transcript
could not say whether the objective was still live, had been completed, or had
never existed. A feature whose entire job is telling the player where they are,
that cannot tell a developer where it is, is undebuggable from the surface it
gets reported on. `commitNode` now logs on the `GOAL` channel EVERY turn —
including turns where nothing moved, because *"still live, model proposed
nothing"* is precisely the observation that separates a stuck objective from an
absent one, and it is invisible if only changes are logged. It reports what the
MODEL said (`saidDone` / `saidClosed` / `proposed`) beside what CODE did
(`before` / `after`): **when those two disagree, the disagreement is the bug.**

**Open — the objective can only end if the STEP model volunteers it.**
`applyLadderDelta` frees the slot on `objectiveDone` / `objectiveClosed`, both
optional fields the mid-turn model must remember to set. That is the same shape
this codebase has now measured failing twice (`hear`, 4 of 2,110 options;
`addGoals`, 0 of 95 turns), and the consequence here is that an objective about
Vic and a pylon survives into a scene about Mel and her ledgers, in a different
place with different people — which reads to the player as the goal not
regenerating. **`runSceneClose` never sees the objective at all** (zero
references in `runSceneClose.ts` or `sceneClose.ts`), even though it is the one
call with the whole scene in view and already the "code decides, model phrases"
seam for history, clocks, cast and canon.

**BUILT 2026-08-11.** Pass the live objective into the close and
require a verdict — achieved / overtaken-and-why / still standing — the same
way the close is already required to produce a history line. That puts the
judgement at the boundary where "the world moved on" is legitimate, keeps
`LIFE-GOAL-3`'s protection against silent mid-scene replacement intact, and
makes regeneration a consequence of a call that always happens rather than of a
field the model may omit. A code-owned backstop (close the objective after N
scenes with "the moment passed") was the weaker alternative and was NOT taken:
deterministic, but it cannot tell an achieved objective from an abandoned one.

**As shipped.** `SCENE_CLOSE_SCHEMA` gains `objectiveVerdict`
(`achieved` | `overtaken` | `stands`) in its **`required`** list — that word is
the whole mechanism, and the reason this works where the step-side fields did
not. `objectiveBecause` stays OPTIONAL, because a required reason would be
invented on every `stands`. The verdict passes through
`ladderDeltaFromVerdict` into the SAME `applyLadderDelta` the step path uses,
so there is one set of rules about what may happen to an objective, and the
freed slot is written in the same `updateLifeWorld` as the history line — a
world cannot end up with the day recorded and the objective still standing.

**Three refusals, each of which keeps a failure recoverable:**
`overtaken` with no reason degrades to `stands` (a goal that vanishes with
nothing to show the player is precisely the silent drop `LIFE-GOAL-3` forbids —
it is the verdict wearing a disguise); an unrecognised verdict is `stands`
(the parse must not lean on the schema holding); and a close that returns no
verdict at all defaults to `stands`. In every ambiguous case the objective
SURVIVES, because keeping it is the recoverable error and deleting it is not.

**A test that had to be rewritten, recorded because it nearly shipped:** the
reasonless-close case was first asserted through `applyLadderDelta`, and passed
with the guard deleted — the reducer independently ignores an empty reason. It
now asserts `ladderDeltaFromVerdict` directly. A test that survives the removal
of the code it names is not testing that code.

**Not treated as a defect:** the same transcript's chapter 1 lasting a single
scene. Owner: *"that's kind of okay every now and then."*

### `LIFE-GOAL-9` — what we are NOT taking from v2

- **The v2 engine and its UI.** Owner's call: keep v1's engine, bring the ideas
  across. The branch's own handover agrees about the UI (*"pretty much no point
  taking any of your UI stuff"*) — it blanks the whole screen between turns and
  loses the header, menu, view modes and debug overlay.
- **`front`-as-blocker.** Deleted on that branch for becoming one unvarying
  string under chip after chip, cleared by a move you could take anyway. A block
  phrased the same way every time and cleared the same way every time is a
  recurring notice, not a decision.
- **The consistency workstream** — already ours. Canon ledger, deterministic
  `npcId` attribution, story log, `disambiguation` + `whyWrong` all landed in v1
  already; that half of the branch is done.
- **Two claimed "pre-existing v1 defects" from the handover, both CHECKED and
  neither real here**: v1's `normaliseToken` already folds accents (so "café"
  does not score as `caf`), and v1 has no full-width eyebrow row to misalign
  against the chip column. Recorded because copying them on the strength of the
  handover's word would have been two changes to code that is already correct.

### `LIFE-GOAL-10` — the gate, before any of this is believed

`BENCH-1` landed 2026-08-10, so the ruler exists. **Median of ≥3 runs, two
fixtures, before/after** — and the reason is v2's single most valuable finding:
identical code scored **3.38 and 2.38** on consecutive 24-turn runs, while
re-judging one fixed transcript spread 0.00. The runs differ, not the judge.
Every conclusion drawn from a single run on that branch, and on ours, was drawn
with an instrument ±0.5 wide.

**Do not ship any of this on the strength of one good-looking playthrough.**

### `LIFE-GOAL-10` — RESULT, 2026-08-11: not shown to differ, and the instrument cannot see most of the feature

Ran as specified: **median of 3 runs, two LONG fixtures, before vs after**, the
before arm from a worktree at `d13d3db` (verified to contain no `goalLadder.ts`
and no `<goalDirective>` — a leak there would have made both arms identical and
produced a fake null). 6 full benchmark runs, 352 calls each, **$0.32 per run,
~$1.94 total** — cheap enough that no story change should ever again ship on one
run.

| | Alice (village) before → after | Ines (apprentice) before → after |
|---|---|---|
| COMPOSITE | 2.29 → 2.40 (**+0.11**) | 2.31 → 2.25 (**−0.06**) |
| INTENT | 2.00 → 1.88 (**−0.12**) | 1.88 → 2.00 (**+0.12**) |
| OPT-FREEDOM | 1.63 → 1.75 (**+0.12**) | 1.88 → 1.63 (**−0.25**) |

**Verdict: NOT SHOWN TO DIFFER, in either direction.** Every delta is smaller
than its own dimension's measured run-to-run spread (COMPOSITE 0.13–0.31,
INTENT 0.13–0.38, OPT-FREEDOM 0.13–0.75), **and the two fixtures disagree on
SIGN for all three headline dimensions** — which is what noise looks like, not
what an effect looks like. The v2 finding this gate was built on reproduced on
our own harness: three runs of identical code scored 2.09 / 2.40 / 2.40.

**The finding that matters more than the numbers: the benchmark is headless, so
it cannot see most of what was built.** It drives the engine and the prompts, so
the only part of the goal feature in scope was the prompt half —
`<goalDirective>` and the ladder state in the step prompt. The deadline chip,
the opening modal, the scene-close restate, the `LIFE-NEED-3.1` block row and
its door are **not exercised at all**. So this result says the prompt half does
no measurable harm; it says nothing whatever about the half that addresses the
report this feature exists for (*"I couldn't tell what I was supposed to do"*),
because that half is invisible to the instrument.

**What follows.** Do not read this as "the goal ladder does not work" — it is
untested, not disproven. The comprehension claim needs a different instrument:
a human playing it, or a judged dimension that asks "could the player tell what
they were being asked to do", which does not currently exist. Adding one is the
prerequisite to ever gating this feature honestly.

**Also recorded:** `ENDING` and `CLICHE-NAME` spread a full 2.00 across three
runs of unchanged code. Neither is usable as a scored dimension at N=3 and both
should be read as pass/fail or given many more runs.

### `LIFE-ENGINE-CMP-4` — the order this forces

1. **Fix the ruler** (`BENCH-1`, median-of-N). Nothing story-shaped can be
   accepted or rejected before this, in either engine.
2. **Then the changes measurable by CONTRACT metrics**, which do not need the
   ruler: SI size, retry rate, precedence.
3. **Only then** anything justified by a judged score — including the deadline,
   the trilemma, and exposure, all of which are open questions rather than
   answers.

**Nothing in v1 changes on the strength of this row.**

### `LIFE-PRECEDENCE-1` — tried, not shown, reverted

An **AGENDA** (three lines on what the narrator is for) and a **PRECEDENCE**
line (*what the player just did > what this turn was asked for > what the scene
is about > everything else*) were added at the top of the `life_step` SI, and
then removed. Recorded rather than deleted, because the reasoning still looks
sound and someone will propose it again.

**The case for it, which has not been refuted:** `step.ts` states hundreds of
rules and nothing in it says which wins when two disagree, which is why three
rewrites of one collision moved nothing (`LIFE-CONTRACT-EX-9`). That argument
stands. What failed is the evidence, not the idea.

**The measurement, 4 runs pooled per arm** (`LIFE-BENCH-8`; the split was
verified on the wire — 4 archived SIs carrying the block, 4 without):

| arm | retried | rate | 95% CI |
|---|---|---|---|
| baseline | 25/121 | 20.7% | [14.4%, 28.7%] |
| + agenda & precedence | 23/119 | **19.3%** | [13.2%, 27.3%] |

Attribution barely moved either (`question-omits-lead-in` 21 → 19). **Intervals
overlap almost completely: nothing was shown.**

**State the limit honestly, because it cuts the other way too.** At n≈120 per
arm this instrument resolves roughly a halving and nothing finer, so this is
*not shown to help*, NOT *shown not to help*. A modest real improvement would be
invisible here. The revert follows the rule set before the run — overlap means
revert — and the rule exists so that prompt additions cannot accumulate on
plausibility alone. **Re-open only with an arm large enough to resolve it:
several hundred `life_step` calls per side, i.e. ~15 short runs each.**

**What was KEPT from the attempt:** `levelLadder.ts`, a mechanical extraction of
the language-level block that the 600-line cap forced. Verified byte-neutral by
regenerating the SI and comparing it to an archived pre-refactor run — identical
to the character. The parallel v2 branch had factored the same block out under
the same name independently.

### Design decision (2026-09-29) — life-sim data-shape compat removed (DEAD-13)

The owner's rule: every client ≥ 0.3.0, prod data migrated by a batch job, no
compatibility code. Removed: the `rollingSummary` shim (`LIFE-MEM-2`, retired),
the `worldMode` optionality (now required on `ZLifeWorld` and on
`createLifeWorld`'s input; the UI toggle default `DEFAULT_WORLD_MODE` is the
onboarding default, not a read fallback), the legacy `viewMode` echo on wurlds
(`LIFE-LANG-3`), and the `/life`, `/life/overview`, `/life/start`,
`/life/:worldId` redirect routes in `src/routes.tsx` — deep links only ever
came from the same server's push payloads and the ~8 testers' bookmarks; the
branded `/wurlds*` routes are the only ones. *Trigger for revisiting:* a push
payload found to still carry a `/life/` path (the fix is the payload, not a
route).
